  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The chapter synthesizes findings from experimental studies on social dilemmas, emphasizing that cooperation is more common than predicted by strong free-riding hypotheses, and that structural features such as group size, information, communication, and institutional rules (including punishment and reward) shape cooperation. Punishment is discussed as a mechanism that can increase cooperation, but its effectiveness depends on design features such as who can punish, the cost, and the identification of punishers and recipients. The chapter does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but notes that higher cooperation is often associated with institutional features like communication and targeted punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This chapter provides useful theoretical and qualitative evidence about the mechanisms by which punishment, communication, and information affect cooperation in public goods games and related social dilemmas. It does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence about efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it report experimental results with efficiency as a dependent variable. For prediction tasks, the chapter supports the idea that punishment can increase cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency), but also warns that unconstrained punishment may reduce cooperation. The review is most useful for understanding which design dimensions are likely to moderate the effect of punishment, rather than for direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Greiff, M. (2013). Rewards and the private provision of public goods on dynamic networks. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The model demonstrates that in a repeated public goods game with rewards (where giving rewards is costly but beneficial to the receiver), the emergence of a social institution prescribing prosocial behavior (contribute and reward contributors) can lead to efficient public good provision, provided that rewards are only given to contributors and the network structure evolves quickly enough to allow exclusion of non-contributors. If rewards are also given to defectors, or if players update strategies too quickly, the system converges to low contribution and low efficiency. The model does not analyze punishment directly, but exclusion from rewards via network dynamics acts as an endogenous sanction. The findings are theoretical and simulation-based, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with reward mechanisms, especially in dynamic network settings. It provides strong theoretical evidence that reward-based institutions can achieve high efficiency if rewards are targeted only at contributors and if the network allows for exclusion of non-contributors. However, it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment, nor does it compare reward to punishment directly. For prediction tasks focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that endogenous exclusion (a form of sanction) can substitute for explicit punishment, but does not quantify the effect of punishment per se. The model's results are most applicable to games with dynamic partner selection and reward, not to standard fixed-group punishment treatments.

- source: Schnell, E., & Muthukrishna, M. (2024). Indirect reciprocity undermines indirect reciprocity destabilizing large- scale cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a nested PGG environment with both local and global public goods, indirect reciprocity (reputation-based conditional aid) can stabilize cooperation at a single scale, but when multiple scales are present, local cooperation is more stable and can invade global cooperation. This occurs even when the global PGG has a higher theoretical maximum payoff. The model demonstrates that the presence of multiple, potentially conflicting reputations leads to a situation where local cooperation undermines global cooperation, reducing overall efficiency at the larger scale. The more fragmented the population (more local groups), the harder it is for global cooperation to be stable. Thus, indirect reciprocity, as typically modeled, cannot by itself sustain efficient, large-scale cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the structure of group membership and the scale at which reputational incentives operate are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from indirect reciprocity-based 'punishment' (here, conditional aid/reward) in PGG-like environments. Specifically, if the game design allows for multiple, nested groups and reputations can be built at the local level, enabling local cooperation, then enabling indirect reciprocity (punishment/reward) may not increase, and can even decrease, efficiency at the global (large-group) level. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of multiple group scales and the ability to build local reputations are key negative moderators of the efficiency effect of punishment/reward mechanisms. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the control (no punishment/reward) game is inefficient, but the addition of indirect reciprocity does not guarantee higher efficiency at the largest scale.

- source: Zschache, J. (2012). Producing public goods in networks: Some effects of social comparison and endogenous network change. *Social Networks*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games on networks, the ability of agents to change their social ties in response to social comparison (i.e., to seek out like-minded cooperators and avoid defectors) can sustain or increase cooperation and thus the production of public goods. Low network density and low costs of network change (low cohesion) allow cooperators to persist by forming supportive subgroups, leading to higher efficiency. High initial cooperation and low segregation also promote full cooperation. When network change is costly (high cohesion), cooperation collapses and efficiency is low. The model shows that both forward-looking and backward-looking (adaptive) agents can achieve high efficiency under favorable network conditions, but backward-looking agents require even more favorable starting conditions. Explicit costly punishment is not modeled; instead, social comparison provides a form of endogenous, non-costly social pressure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that endogenous network change and social comparison can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public goods games. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that game designs allowing for dynamic partner selection or network rewiring (low cohesion/costs) will see higher efficiency, especially when initial cooperation is high and the network is not highly segregated. However, the absence of explicit costly punishment means the results are most applicable to settings where social comparison or endogenous social pressure is the main incentive, not where formal punishment is enabled. The results are directly informative for predicting efficiency in networked PGGs with endogenous tie change, but less so for classic PGGs with explicit punishment options.

- source: Salahshour, M. (2021). Evolution of cooperation in costly institutions exhibits Red Queen and Black Queen dynamics in heterogeneous public goods. *Communications Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model demonstrates that introducing a costly institution (with a participation fee) alongside a free institution in a public goods game can increase cooperation and group efficiency. For low enhancement factors (low profitability), only the costly institution supports cooperation, and average payoffs remain close to the entrance cost (i.e., not much higher than the baseline). For higher enhancement factors, cooperation emerges in both institutions, and group payoffs increase above zero. The costly institution is more likely to be populated by cooperators, as defectors avoid the entrance cost unless there are enough cooperators to exploit. The model also shows that the effect of the entrance cost is context-dependent: it is beneficial for cooperation and efficiency at low profitability, but can be detrimental at high profitability if the cost is too high, as it reduces participation. The results hold in both well-mixed and structured populations, though spatial structure removes some bistability and further supports cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that introducing a costly institution (entrance fee) in a public goods game can increase efficiency and group payoff, especially when the baseline profitability (mpcr/enhancement factor) is low. The entrance cost acts as a filter, keeping defectors out and allowing cooperators to achieve higher payoffs. For prediction tasks, the presence of a costly institution (or, by analogy, a punishment mechanism with a cost to participate) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control with no such cost, particularly when the marginal per-capita return is low. However, if the cost is too high and the game is already highly profitable, the effect may reverse. The model does not include explicit punishment, but the costly institution's effect is adjacent to punishment in that it deters free-riding through a cost barrier. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where institutional participation costs or costly precommitment mechanisms are present, rather than direct peer punishment.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Zhao, J. H., Gu, C. L., & Wang, T. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games under a risk-transfer mechanism. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a risk-transfer (coalition) mechanism into a spatial public goods game can enhance or reduce cooperation and group efficiency, depending on the synergy coefficient (r) and the coalition closeness (alpha). In two-strategy systems, coalitional cooperators are more competitive than traditional cooperators, and coalitional defectors are less competitive than traditional defectors. In four-strategy systems, increasing coalition closeness (alpha) generally increases cooperation, but can also lead to non-monotonic effects where too strong coalitions reduce cooperation due to the breakdown of indirect territorial competition. The mechanism acts as a wealth redistribution system, strengthening cooperators at the boundary of clusters but sometimes making them vulnerable to invasion by other types. The model does not include punishment or reward, but the coalition mechanism is compared to these in the discussion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency in PGGs, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides informative theoretical evidence about how payoff-redistribution mechanisms (such as coalitions or risk-sharing) can affect group efficiency and cooperation in spatial PGGs. The findings suggest that mechanisms which redistribute payoffs among players can have complex, sometimes non-monotonic effects on efficiency, depending on the structure of the game (e.g., synergy coefficient, spatial structure, coalition closeness). For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of any payoff-redistribution mechanism (including punishment, if it were present) may depend strongly on the details of the mechanism and the spatial or network structure. However, direct quantitative prediction of punishment effects is not possible from this paper.

- source: Salahshour, M. (2021). Evolution of cooperation and consistent personalities in public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in two-round public goods games, both reward-based and assortative mechanisms can promote the evolution of cooperation and the emergence of consistent cooperative or defective personalities. In the 'reward dilemma' scenario, only first-round cooperators are allowed to play a second PGG, which can incentivize cooperation if the second game's enhancement factor is high enough. In the 'assortative' scenario, both cooperators and defectors play a second PGG in separate groups, and competition between these groups can further promote cooperation and personality consistency. The model shows that cooperation is maximized at intermediate values of the second-round enhancement factor, and that consistent personalities (cooperating or defecting in both rounds) naturally evolve. However, the paper does not model punishment or sanctions, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding how reward and assortment mechanisms (and the possibility of repeated or conditional interaction) can promote cooperation and personality consistency, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or their efficiency effects. The findings may be indirectly informative for prediction tasks involving reward or assortment, but not for punishment. The model's outcomes are primarily behavioral (cooperation rates, personality consistency), not efficiency or group payoff, so it should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Kang, H. W., Li, Z. K., Shen, Y., Huang, Z. Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2024). From eligibility to suitability: Regulation and restriction of reputation-based access system on free-riding behavior in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that reputation-based exclusion mechanisms, which filter out low-reputation (likely free-riding) individuals before the PGG round, can significantly increase cooperation and total system payoff in spatial public goods games. The homogeneous exclusion model requires higher exclusion probability to match the performance of traditional (post-game) exclusion, but both converge to high cooperation and payoff at high exclusion probability. The heterogeneous exclusion model, which tailors exclusion probability to the reputation gap, outperforms both the homogeneous and traditional models in promoting cooperation and system payoff at high exclusion intensity. Both reputation-based models are especially effective at suppressing second-order free-riding, a limitation of traditional exclusion. The models are simulated on a 100x100 lattice with three strategies (C, D, EC), and results are robust across a range of synergy factors and exclusion costs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that pre-game, reputation-based exclusion mechanisms (a form of access control) can increase group payoff and cooperation in spatial PGGs, especially when exclusion is sufficiently strict. The findings suggest that, for games with spatial structure and reputation tracking, enabling such exclusion mechanisms is likely to increase efficiency relative to control (no exclusion or punishment). The effect is stronger for the heterogeneous (adaptive) exclusion model. The results are most directly applicable to spatial PGGs with continuous contribution, reputation tracking, and exclusion as the main sanctioning mechanism. The paper does not study classic costly punishment (where players pay to reduce others' payoffs post-game), so direct transfer to standard punishment-enabled PGGs should be cautious. However, the results are highly informative for predicting the effect of exclusion-based access control on efficiency, especially in spatial/reputation contexts.

- source: Takács, K., & Janky, B. (2007). Smiling contributions:: Social control in a public goods game with network decline. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper develops a theoretical framework for public goods games with endogenous network change and social control mechanisms. It predicts that clustered networks reduce collective action and that the ability to delete ties leads to segregation between contributors and defectors. Social control is operationalized as the ability to send happy or sad smileys, which are intended to induce conformity or selective incentives. The experimental design is described, but no data or results are presented. The main dependent variable in the proposed experiments is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how network structure and non-costly social control (smileys) might affect contribution behavior in public goods games. However, it does not provide empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it study costly punishment as typically defined in the literature. The model suggests that social control (even if non-costly) and network clustering can influence cooperation, but the lack of empirical results and the focus on non-payoff outcomes limit its direct utility for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions. The design dimensions are discussed in the context of the model and proposed experiment, but not tested empirically.

- source: Yang, R., Chen, T., & Chen, Q. (2020). Promoting cooperation by reputation-based payoff transfer mechanism in public goods game. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The reputation-based payoff transfer mechanism, which redistributes payoffs from low- to high-reputation players, can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial public goods game. The effect depends on the parameters controlling the proportion of players who lose or gain payoffs (transfer ratios) and the strength of the transfer. When both the proportion of punished (transferred) and rewarded (accepted) players are moderate and balanced, cooperation can quickly dominate the population. The mechanism is most effective when all players are either punished or rewarded (transfer ratio = 1). However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms combining endogenous punishment and reward based on reputation can increase cooperation rates in public goods games, especially when the redistribution is balanced and covers the whole population. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings may be useful for predicting cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes as required by the downstream task.

- source: Toriumi, F., Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2020). A belief in rewards accelerates cooperation on consumer-generated media. *Journal of Computational Social Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a public goods game with only rewards and meta-rewards (no punishment), cooperation can be sustained if players can observe and respond to the individual meta-rewarding rates of others. When players base their reward-giving on the observed likelihood of receiving a meta-reward from a specific cooperator, stable cooperation emerges. If players cannot condition on this information, cooperation collapses. The benefit-to-cost ratio of rewards and meta-rewards must be sufficiently high for cooperation to be maintained. The model does not include punishment, and efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding the role of reward and meta-reward mechanisms, and highlights the importance of observability and benefit-to-cost ratios for sustaining cooperation. However, since punishment is not modeled, and efficiency or group payoff is not a reported outcome, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for understanding adjacent mechanisms (reward-based), but not for the core prediction task.

- source: Xiao, J. F., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2023). Evolution of cooperation driven by sampling reward. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the sampling reward mechanism can effectively increase the average cooperation level in both PGG and CRD models. Higher reward thresholds and higher reward intensities promote cooperation, but also increase the cost to the institution. The effect of reward intensity is non-linear: above a critical threshold, further increases yield little additional cooperation. Sample size has a non-monotonic effect on cost, with intermediate sizes minimizing cost. The findings are robust across both game types. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that reward mechanisms (specifically, sampling reward) can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, with effects moderated by reward threshold, intensity, and sample size. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper is most useful for understanding how reward design parameters affect cooperation rates and institutional costs, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. It does not provide empirical or theoretical results on punishment, nor does it analyze efficiency as defined by group payoff relative to full cooperation.

- source: Chen, T., Wu, Z. H., & Wang, L. (2018). Disseminators or silencers: The effect of information diffusion intensity on cooperation in public goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and type of information dissemination strongly affect the speed at which full cooperation is achieved in a spatial public goods game with reputation and conformity. Negative disseminators (who spread information about defectors) most often lead to the fastest convergence to full cooperation, followed by neutral and positive disseminators. Silencers (no dissemination) are least effective. The effect of dissemination type is most pronounced when conformity synergy, link-neighbor degree, population size, or environmental noise are high, or when the synergy factor is low. However, all models eventually reach full cooperation, so there is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare differences between models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the role of information diffusion and reputation in accelerating the achievement of cooperation. The findings suggest that mechanisms which amplify reputational consequences for defectors (akin to negative gossip or public shaming) can speed up the transition to full cooperation, especially in certain parameter regimes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not address whether the final payoffs or efficiency differ between conditions.

- source: Wang, M. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2020). The interplay of behaviors and attitudes in public goods game considering environmental investment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The introduction of an alliance strategy, where players pay an aid fee to help exploited allies, leads to a dramatic increase in the prevalence of cooperative behavior (C + A) in spatial PGGs. Alliance clusters are robust to defector invasion and can expand to dominate the population, especially for intermediate values of the synergy factor (r) and aid intensity (beta). The cooperation level is a non-monotonic function of both r and beta, with optimal intervals for each. Average payoffs per strategy are reported, showing that alliance players eventually outperform defectors and cooperators. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total welfare as a function of the alliance mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms analogous to mutual aid or alliance (distinct from punishment) can promote cooperation and increase the prevalence of cooperative strategies in spatial PGGs. However, because the model does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare, it is only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency effects of punishment. The findings suggest that payoff redistribution among cooperators can be highly effective in suppressing defection, but the results cannot be directly transferred to settings with explicit punishment. The paper is most useful for understanding the effects of mutual aid or alliance-type mechanisms, not punishment.

- source: Podder, S., & Righi, S. (2023). Complexity of Behavioural Strategies and Cooperation in the Optional Public Goods Game. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in the OPGG, increasing the complexity of behavioral strategies (i.e., the ability to condition actions on more finely-grained reputational information) increases the proportion of cooperative actions, especially under social norms that assign different reputations to defectors and loners. However, this effect plateaus after a certain level of complexity. Cooperation is higher in smaller groups, and the benefit of strategy complexity is most pronounced in these settings. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The results suggest that, in the absence of punishment, reputation-based mechanisms combined with complex behavioral strategies can sustain cooperation, but the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes rather than efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding how reputation systems and behavioral strategy complexity affect cooperation rates in OPGG settings without punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that, in the absence of punishment, increasing behavioral strategy complexity and using social norms that distinguish defectors from loners can increase cooperation rates, especially in small groups. However, it does not inform how punishment would affect efficiency or payoff, nor does it provide control or treatment efficiency values.

- source: Liu, Q., Xu, Z. J., & Zhang, L. Z. (2018). Evolutionary Public Goods Game on Evolving Random Networks. *Journal of the Korean Physical Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing informed cooperators to rewire their network connections at a cost can substantially increase the prevalence of cooperation in public goods games on random networks. Higher rates of network rewiring (by informed cooperators) lower the threshold for cooperation to emerge, especially under adverse conditions (low synergy factor). The system exhibits distinct phases (full cooperation, full defection, coexistence, and segregated sub-networks), and the transition between these phases depends on the synergy factor and rewiring rate. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not implement or analyze punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Instead, it studies the effect of costly information and network rewiring on cooperation rates. While the findings suggest that network adaptation can promote cooperation, they do not provide evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor about the effect of punishment. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency, though it may be contextually relevant for understanding alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Yang, R. (2019). Using rewards reasonably: The effects of stratified-rewards in public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that stratified-rewards can promote, maintain, or even inhibit efficiency (measured as available funds for public goods) depending on the scope and distribution of rewards. If the number of people who receive rewards is small and the number who get high-level rewards is controlled, available funds can increase. If too many people get high rewards, available funds can fall below the baseline, even if all players cooperate. The sum of contributions and the standard deviation of contributions are also affected by reward parameters. The fraction of cooperators is always high with rewards, but this does not guarantee high efficiency. The optimal reward design balances the number of recipients and the cost of rewards to maximize available funds.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of reward mechanisms on efficiency in PGG-like environments is highly sensitive to the design of the reward scheme. It shows that simply enabling rewards does not guarantee higher efficiency; the number of rewarded players, the proportion of high-level rewards, and the cost of rewards all interact to determine the net group payoff. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of a reward mechanism can have positive, neutral, or negative effects on efficiency, depending on these design details. Since the paper does not study punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of punishment, but it does highlight the importance of cost structures and incentive distribution in predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Quek, H. Y., Tan, K. C., & Tay, A. (2009). Public Goods Provision: An Evolutionary Game Theoretic Study Under Asymmetric Information. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Intelligence and Ai in Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that information asymmetry generally reduces cooperation and welfare compared to homogeneous information settings. Restricting contribution choices (all-or-nothing) and using provision point mechanisms (thresholds for public good provision) can increase efficiency and align welfare across types, especially when both are combined. Reward schemes that select individuals based on accumulated group achievements (rather than group-based rewards) are more effective at increasing welfare and discouraging free riding. Larger group sizes and population sizes have complex effects, with larger populations amplifying both cooperative and defecting tendencies depending on the information structure. The paper does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how information structure, action space granularity, provision mechanisms, and reward/selection schemes affect efficiency in public goods games. However, it does not include any form of punishment or sanctioning, so it cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It is useful for understanding baseline efficiency and the impact of other design dimensions, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., & Chen, Z. Q. (2016). The public goods game with a new form of shared reward. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a shared reward, proportional to the number of cooperators, can enable cooperation to invade and become stable in a population of defectors if the reward parameter exceeds a critical threshold. This threshold depends negatively on the population size and the public goods multiplier, and positively on the group size. Larger populations and higher public goods multipliers make it easier for cooperation to be favored, while larger group sizes make it harder. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, and thus does not directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. However, it does provide theoretical insight into how reward mechanisms (distinct from punishment) can affect the evolution of cooperation and group payoff. For the downstream prediction task focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but may be useful for understanding the comparative role of reward-based interventions.

- source: Reeves, T., Ohtsuki, H., & Fukui, S. (2017). Asymmetric public goods game cooperation through pest control. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric public goods games (modeled as pest control among farmers), the Nash equilibrium investment and resulting efficiency depend strongly on the discount factor and the number of players. There are threshold effects: only farmers close to the risk (frontier) contribute when the discount factor is low, while more distant farmers contribute only if the discount factor is high enough. Increasing the number of players does not always increase efficiency; in some parameter regions, adding more players can paradoxically reduce total investment and group welfare. Asymmetry leads to a disproportionate burden on those at greatest risk, making the system more vulnerable to shocks. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence for how efficiency in asymmetric public goods games depends on player count, discount factor, and the structure of risk, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is highly informative for baseline (control) efficiency in asymmetric, no-punishment settings, especially regarding the effects of group size and discounting. However, it does not inform the effect of enabling punishment, as no such mechanism is present or analyzed. Its findings suggest that efficiency can be non-monotonic in group size and is sensitive to asymmetry and time preferences, which should be considered when predicting control efficiency, but it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment.

- source: Wang, X. J., & Chen, W. M. (2019). The evolution of cooperation in public good game with deposit. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a deposit mechanism into the PGG can promote cooperation under certain parameter regimes. The equilibrium frequencies of cooperation, deposit cooperation, and defection depend on the deposit size, discount rate, benefit-sharing rule (weak vs. strong altruism), and the threshold for public good provision. Higher thresholds and weak altruism (where contributors also benefit) increase the equilibrium level of cooperation. Lower deposit and discount rates promote deposit cooperation. The model provides explicit formulas for expected payoffs and equilibrium frequencies, but does not address punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments. It is not informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, as it does not include any punishment, sanction, or peer enforcement mechanism. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how deposit-based commitment mechanisms and benefit-sharing rules affect cooperation and payoffs in PGGs without punishment.

- source: Tu, J. (2018). Contribution inequality in the spatial public goods game: Should the rich contribute more?. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when the rich contribute more (higher w), the effect on average wealth (group payoff) and cooperation rate depends on the enhancement factor (mpcr, or r/(z+1)). For low enhancement factors, higher w reduces both cooperation and average wealth. For intermediate enhancement, higher w can increase average wealth even as cooperation rate falls, because the extra contributions from the rich compensate for more defectors. For high enhancement, higher w increases both cooperation and average wealth, and reduces wealth inequality. The relationship between cooperation rate and group payoff is not always positive: higher cooperation does not always mean higher group wealth. The results are robust to population size but sensitive to network degree.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not study punishment or sanctions, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. However, it provides strong evidence that group payoff (efficiency) can be decoupled from cooperation rate, especially when contribution rules are heterogeneous. The findings suggest that interventions that change contribution rules (e.g., progressive contributions) can have complex effects on efficiency, depending on the enhancement factor. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant to punishment, but it is highly relevant to understanding how efficiency responds to other game design dimensions, especially mpcr and contribution heterogeneity.

- source: Sun, C. H., & Duan, H. B. (2015). Impact of shill intervention on the evolution of cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The introduction of shill agents with a Fixed-Cost-per-Player contribution rule into a PGG on a scale-free network can substantially increase both cooperation rates and average payoffs (social welfare), particularly when shills are preferentially placed on high-degree nodes and the dilemma is severe (low enhancement factor). The effect is strongest for high-degree-preference shill placement (large alpha) and in harsh environments. As the environment becomes more favorable (higher enhancement factor), local cooperators reappear and can coexist with shill cooperators, and distributing shills on low-degree nodes (small alpha) can maximize social welfare. The paper provides detailed simulation evidence on how these effects depend on the enhancement factor, shill fraction, and shill placement, but does not study punishment or sanctioning.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments with external intervention via shill agents, but not for environments with punishment or sanctioning. The findings suggest that introducing agents with alternative contribution rules (partial free-riders) can increase efficiency in harsh environments, especially if they are placed on network hubs. The results are parameter-dependent and highlight the importance of network structure and agent placement. However, since the paper does not involve punishment, its direct predictive value for punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency is limited. It is most useful for understanding the effects of external intervention and heterogeneity in contribution rules on efficiency.

- source: Olejarz, J. W., & Nowak, M. A. (2014). Evolution of staying together in the context of diffusible public goods. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in public goods games with diffusible goods can evolve if the benefit-to-cost ratio exceeds a threshold determined by the probability of staying together (q) and the internal absorption efficiency (alpha_k). Clustering increases the efficiency of cooperation by allowing cooperators to preferentially benefit from their own public goods. The model also shows that if defectors and cooperators differ in their ability to absorb public goods, coexistence or bistability can occur. Introducing a cost to large clusters (gamma_k) can create an optimal cluster size for cooperation. The model provides explicit analytical conditions for these outcomes, but does not consider punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how structural and spatial factors (such as clustering and internal absorption efficiency) affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency in public goods games. However, it does not address punishment or sanctioning, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in control games without punishment, or in environments where clustering and spatial structure are the main mechanisms supporting cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how efficiency depends on clustering, internal benefit allocation, and the benefit-to-cost ratio, but offers no evidence about punishment effects.

- source: Liu, P. H., & Liu, J. (2017). Contribution diversity and incremental learning promote cooperation in public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that both contribution diversity (CD)—where individuals reallocate their contributions based on response rates—and incremental learning (IL)—where individuals imitate those with long-term high payoffs—promote cooperation in PGGs on scale-free networks. CD is more effective at higher mpcr (eta), while IL stabilizes cooperation even when CD is less effective. The combination of CD and IL is most effective. The main reported outcomes are cooperation frequency and the average payoff/fitness difference between cooperators and defectors. No group efficiency or total group payoff is reported. The models do not include punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs, as punishment is not modeled. It does provide indirect evidence that mechanisms other than punishment (contribution diversity and incremental learning) can promote cooperation and increase the relative payoff of cooperators, especially at higher mpcr. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative. For tasks considering alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms, the findings suggest that structural and learning modifications can substitute for punishment in promoting cooperation, but the effect on group efficiency is not quantified.

- source: Amado, A., Huang, W. N., Campos, P. R. A., & Ferreira, F. F. (2015). Learning process in public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with bounded rationality and finite memory, both the learning process (dynamic vs static) and the public goods enhancement factor (r) strongly affect cooperation rates. In dynamic learning, increasing r leads to a transition from defection to a neutral regime (cooperation ~50%), while in static learning, cooperation can exceed 50% when r > N. Memory size smooths the transition from defection to cooperation. The expected payoff change is positive when r > 1 and cooperation is present, but the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff as a main outcome. No punishment or reward mechanisms are included.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide detailed modeling of how learning, memory, and the enhancement factor r affect cooperation and expected payoffs in standard PGGs. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide useful context for baseline (no-punishment) efficiency as a function of r, memory, and learning process.

- source: Wang, C. Q., Lin, Z. Z., & Rothman, D. S. (2022). Public goods game on coevolving networks driven by the similarity and difference of payoff. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the way agents update strategies—locally, globally, or a mix—has complex effects on cooperation and payoffs in spatial PGGs. When both cooperators and defectors have the same tendency for global updating, local updating is more beneficial for cooperation at low synergy factors (r), while both local and global updating can support cooperation at high r, but their combination can be detrimental. When cooperators and defectors differ in their global updating propensity, the system's cooperation and payoff outcomes depend sensitively on these propensities. Moderate global updating by defectors and low global updating by cooperators most favors cooperation and higher payoffs. The paper does not study punishment or its effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGGs. It is useful for understanding how information structure (local vs global updating) and network effects influence cooperation and payoffs, but does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled versus punishment-disabled efficiency. The absence of punishment mechanisms means it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Wang, J. W., Chen, W., Yu, F. Y., He, J. L., & Xu, W. S. (2022). Wealth-based rule favors cooperation in costly public goods games when individual selection is inevitable. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in both independent and interdependent PGGs with two cooperative strategies, the coexistence and prevalence of each strategy depend on the synergy factor and the difference in input costs. In the interdependent model, effective input and economic benefit are maximized when the two strategies are present in similar proportions. The interdependence mechanism (In-PGG) can sometimes lead to more quasi-cooperation than the independent mechanism (An-PGG), especially when the synergy factor and the difference in input costs are small. However, the paper does not directly report or quantify group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the stable proportions of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctions. It is useful for understanding how the structure of cooperation (independent vs interdependent strategies) and parameter settings affect the prevalence of cooperative behaviors, but it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes in a way that can be directly mapped to the prediction task. The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how structural features of the game (such as interdependence of contributions) affect the emergence of cooperation, but not for the effect of punishment.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in a public goods game with environmental investment is promoted when social attention (driven by individual attitudes and environmental feedback) is high and when the investment budget exceeds a threshold. The model identifies three types of public response mechanisms to environmental degradation, with the 'precautionary' mechanism (rapid response to early degradation) being most beneficial for cooperation. The level of cooperation is sensitive to the parameters governing attention and forgetfulness, but the level of social attention itself is robust to these parameters within certain intervals. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present, and efficiency or group payoff is not analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its findings are limited to the dynamics of cooperation and social attention in a PGG with environmental investment and evolving attitudes. It may provide indirect context for how non-punitive mechanisms (like attention or investment) affect cooperation, but is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Battu, B., & Srinivasan, N. (2020). Evolution of conditional cooperation in public good games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that high and stable cooperation (measured by donation rates) can be achieved in repeated public good games when agents' social learning is sufficiently biased toward prestige (social value) or when both prestige and payoff are weighted but with some noise. When imitation is driven mainly by payoff, cooperation collapses. The model does not include punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding how social learning mechanisms (prestige vs. payoff) affect the evolution of cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public good games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that cooperation can be sustained by prestige-biased social learning even in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper does not provide usable quantitative or qualitative evidence about the efficiency impact of punishment or about moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Tomassini, M., & Antonioni, A. (2021). Computational behavioral models in public goods games with migration between groups. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that allowing unsatisfied players to migrate between groups increases average contribution in public goods games, especially for lower enhancement factors (r). The effect of group size and update timing (synchronous vs asynchronous) is minor. Heterogeneity in migration propensity has little effect on average contribution compared to a homogeneous population. The amount of information available to players (own payoff/contribution vs also knowing the number of contributors in the group) does not substantially change the results. However, the paper does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on migration as an alternative to punishment may be contextually informative for understanding other forms of exit or mobility, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The reported outcomes are average contribution rates, not efficiency or payoff, and thus are only adjacent to the prediction task.

- source: Ramalingam, A., Morales, A. J., & Walker, J. M. (2018). Varying experimental instructions to improve comprehension: Punishment in public goods games. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games, mechanisms that allow individuals to preferentially imitate high-reputation neighbors (high reputation reasoning ability) promote the emergence and stability of cooperation. However, when reputation is updated using second-order rules (taking into account both the individual's and the worst neighbor's actions), cooperation is inhibited compared to first-order rules, especially when the magnitude of reputation change is large. Heterogeneity in reasoning ability (both endogenous and exogenous) can reduce cooperation, particularly when the variance or degree of heterogeneity is high. The main reported outcome is the cooperation rate; average payoffs are discussed in relation to strategy and reputation clusters but not as a primary outcome. No punishment or sanctioning is modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how reputation-based social learning and information structures affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The reported outcomes (cooperation rate, reputation distribution, and some average payoffs by type) are not directly translatable to group efficiency as defined in the prediction task. Therefore, this paper should not be weighted for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may provide contextual insight into how non-punitive social mechanisms (like reputation) affect cooperation.

  findings: The paper finds that longer memory (up to a threshold) and slower memory decay promote higher levels of cooperation in both scale-free and small-world networks. Heterogeneity in memory length (asynchronous model) further enhances cooperation, especially in scale-free networks. The synergy factor (analogous to MPCR) also increases cooperation. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperator density, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or reward mechanisms are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how memory and network structure affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based outcomes or the impact of punishment. The absence of punishment mechanisms and efficiency outcomes limits its direct relevance to the prediction task.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhang, J. L., Xie, G. M., & Wang, L. (2010). Diversity of game strategies promotes the evolution of cooperation in public goods games. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a continuous range of cooperation degrees (strategy diversity) in public goods games leads to higher average cooperation, especially when all players have equal initial wealth. The transition to high cooperation occurs at lower values of the multiplication factor (mpcr) than in previous models with only two strategies. The presence of strategy diversity prevents long-term exploitation and leads to the extinction of defectors at relatively low mpcr values. However, the paper does not study punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings on strategy diversity and cooperation rates may be contextually informative for understanding how game structure affects cooperation, but it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

  findings: The study finds that when players preferentially imitate neighbors with higher payoffs (higher α), cooperation is promoted and loners become less prevalent. When α is low or negative (preference for low-payoff neighbors), loners dominate. The enhancement factor r (analogous to MPCR) also modulates the prevalence of cooperation, with higher r supporting more cooperation. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

  findings: The paper finds that in multiplex public goods games with limited and partible resources, cooperation is more likely to be maintained when at least one network layer is scale-free (high degree diversity) and when agents use a 'greedy-first' updating rule (imitating strategies in the higher-payoff layer). Homogeneous network structures and 'generous-first' updating rules lead to higher rates of defection and the collapse of cooperation. The study does not consider punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes. However, it is informative about the role of network structure and resource allocation mechanisms in sustaining cooperation in multiplex PGGs. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may provide contextual insight into how structural and behavioral mechanisms (other than punishment) affect cooperation.

  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to preferentially invest in groups with more cooperators (higher alpha) and to use longer memory of past group composition increases the fraction of cooperators in both well-mixed and structured populations. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that allowing individuals to choose between multiple public goods games (resources) with different enhancement factors can promote cooperation. Defectors tend to cluster in the highest quality resource, while cooperators can 'shelter' in lower quality resources, avoiding exploitation. Cooperation is maximized when the two highest quality resources are similar in quality. The model is analyzed both analytically (replicator-mutator dynamics) and via simulation, showing robust results across parameter ranges. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The main outcome is the density of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding how choice between public resources affects cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding the impact of resource choice and group structure on cooperation.

  findings: The study finds that when individual investment ability and enhancement factor are positively correlated (aligned inequality), cooperation is strongly promoted, with the system reaching full cooperation at lower enhancement factors than in the traditional PGG. When these are negatively correlated (misaligned inequality), cooperation is suppressed and may disappear entirely. The mechanism is that high-contribution cooperators form the core of cooperative clusters, attracting and sustaining cooperation among lower-contribution individuals. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. - source: Battu, B., & Rahwan, T. (2023). Cooperation without punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games, cooperation cannot be sustained by conditional cooperators alone, nor by simply injecting altruists if there is no social noise. However, when a moderate level of noise is present, a small but critical fraction of altruists can trigger and sustain high levels of cooperation among conditional cooperators. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and instead focuses on the role of positive reciprocity and social learning. The main outcome is the level of cooperation (contribution rate), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism in its model. Its findings are relevant for understanding alternative mechanisms to punishment for sustaining cooperation, but do not inform the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The paper does, however, argue that punishment can reduce social welfare, but this is a theoretical and literature-based claim, not a result of the presented model. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing context for non-punishment mechanisms, but not as a source of quantitative or comparative evidence on punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Xie, K., Liu, X. W., Chen, H., & Yang, J. (2022). Preferential selection and expected payoff drive cooperation in spatial voluntary public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial VPGG, introducing an expected payoff threshold and preferential selection in strategy updating can significantly increase the cooperation rate under certain parameter regimes. Specifically, positive expected payoff thresholds (p_e) and higher multiplication factors (r) promote cooperation, while the effect of the preferential selection parameter (a) depends on its sign and value. The cooperation rate is robust to a for positive p_e, but both a and r matter for negative p_e. The main dependent variable is the cooperation rate; no efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it does provide detailed evidence on how cooperation rates in a spatial VPGG are affected by expected payoff thresholds, preferential selection, and the multiplication factor. For prediction tasks focused on punishment or efficiency, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be contextually useful for understanding how non-punishment mechanisms (like aspiration levels and neighbor selection) affect cooperation rates.

The time scale ratio of selection to interaction also matters: more frequent interaction relative to selection promotes cooperation. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the existence and maintenance of pure cooperator groups in a multi-group public goods game can promote the rise of cooperation in the population, even without mechanisms like punishment or reward. However, mutation and migration allow defectors to invade cooperator groups, undermining cooperation. Group division (splitting of large groups) can counteract the negative effects of mutation by generating new cooperator groups, but only if mutation rates are not too high and group division is sufficiently frequent. The synergy factor (r) and contribution level (c) also moderate these dynamics. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly transferable to payoff-based prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it provides indirect context on how group structure, mutation, and migration affect cooperation rates. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, except as a reminder that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., group division, migration) can strongly affect cooperation, and that the presence or absence of such mechanisms should be considered when interpreting or predicting the effects of punishment.

- source: Quan, J., Li, H. Z., Zhang, M., & Wang, X. J. (2024). Cooperation dynamics in nonlinear spatial public goods games with endogenous synergy and discounting feedback. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing endogenous feedback—where the payoff structure depends on the current and historical level of cooperation—promotes the emergence and stability of cooperation in spatial public goods games. Both the length and intensity of the feedback mechanism (how much history is considered and how strongly it affects payoffs) increase the fraction of cooperators in the population. The model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The results are based on simulations of cooperation density and strategy dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on endogenous feedback and nonlinear payoffs may be contextually relevant for understanding mechanisms that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Li, Y. P., Hu, X. Y., Jin, X., Zhang, H. Z., Yang, J. J., & Wang, Z. (2025). Environmental information perception enhances cooperation in stochastic public goods games via Q-learning. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a stochastic PGG with environmental feedback, the ability of individuals to perceive environmental state information significantly promotes the emergence and stability of cooperation. Specifically, the critical synergy factor (r) required for cooperation to emerge is lower when individuals can perceive the environment, compared to both a static environment and a dynamic environment that is not perceived. The study uses Q-learning agents on spatial networks and explores a variety of model extensions (update rules, neighborhood size, network topology, etc.), consistently finding that environmental information perception lowers the cooperation threshold. However, the paper does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and does not include any punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are adjacent to the prediction task, as they show that environmental information perception (not punishment) can promote cooperation (not efficiency) in stochastic PGGs. The results may be useful for understanding how information and feedback mechanisms affect cooperation thresholds, but they do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly informative.

- source: Wang, X. W., Jiang, L. L., Nie, S., & Wang, B. H. (2015). Uncovering cooperative behaviors with sparse historical behavior data in the spatial games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to study how conformity-driven behavior affects cooperation in public goods games with binary heterogeneous endowments (rich and poor). The main finding is a 'double-edged sword' effect: when the multiplication factor (synergy of cooperation) is high, conformity enhances cooperation by strengthening network reciprocity; when the multiplication factor is low, conformity inhibits cooperation, especially among the poor, by allowing defection to infiltrate cooperative clusters. These effects are robust to variations in conformity strength, the proportion of rich, and the extension to multiplayer PGGs. However, the study only reports on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on conformity and cooperation rates in heterogeneous endowment settings may be contextually informative for understanding behavioral dynamics in PGGs, but are not directly transferable to efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor to the effects of punishment. Thus, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Liu, A. Z., Zhang, Y. L., Chen, X. J., & Sun, C. Y. (2017). Randomness and diversity matter in the maintenance of the public resources. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that both greater diversity (more available contribution levels) and greater randomness (higher mutation, smaller population, weaker selection) increase the average investment in the public goods game. Under weak selection, average investment is high and insensitive to diversity or randomness. Under strong selection, average investment is low and insensitive to diversity or population size, but increases with mutation. Under intermediate selection, average investment increases with diversity and mutation, and decreases with population size. No payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported, and no punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. It is only relevant for understanding how diversity in contribution options and evolutionary randomness affect average investment in the absence of enforcement mechanisms. It provides no evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency.
