  findings: The paper finds that in threshold public goods games, offering insurance to cooperators (but not defectors) can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies, especially when the compensation for failed games is high and the threshold for success is high. However, if defectors are also allowed to purchase insurance, insured defection can become dominant, leading to a decrease in cooperative behaviors. The network structure (random regular vs. scale-free) also moderates these effects. The main outcomes are the steady-state fractions of each strategy; efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, but can be inferred from the modeled payoffs. The results suggest that insurance can substitute for some risk-mitigating effects of punishment, but the mechanism is distinct.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies insurance as a risk-mitigation mechanism rather than punishment or sanctions. However, it does provide indirect evidence about how risk-mitigation mechanisms (like insurance) can affect cooperation and, by implication, group welfare. The findings suggest that mechanisms which reduce the risk of loss for contributors (such as insurance) can promote cooperation, but if such mechanisms are also available to defectors, they may undermine cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper is most relevant for understanding the effects of risk and insurance, not punishment. It does not provide direct parameterizations for the target game design dimensions related to punishment.

- source: Lee, J. H., Yamaguchi, R., Yokomizo, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2020). Preservation of the value of rice paddy fields: Investigating how to prevent farmers from abandoning the fields by means of evolutionary game theory. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation (maintenance effort) and thus group efficiency can be sustained when responsibility for collective action (maintenance) is local (among neighbors), but not when it is global (all players responsible for all facilities). Maintenance effort promotes cultivation effort, but not vice versa. Spatial structure and local interaction are key to sustaining group welfare. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in nonlinear public goods games with spatial structure and local cooperation, but not to games with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that local responsibility and spatial structure can sustain cooperation and group efficiency in nonlinear public goods games, but it does not inform the effect of punishment or sanctions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative. However, it is highly informative for understanding the baseline efficiency of control games (without punishment) as a function of spatial structure, local vs. global responsibility, and ecological parameters. It can help predict when efficiency will be high or low in the absence of punishment, but not the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Joshi, J., Brännström, & Dieckmann, U. (2020). Emergence of social inequality in the spatial harvesting of renewable public goods. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that when harvesting efficiency is low, all agents evolve to be sedentary and harvest at low rates, resulting in equitable but inefficient resource use. As harvesting efficiency increases, agents become mobile, and resource extraction approaches the maximum sustainable yield, achieving both efficiency and equity. Further increases in harvesting efficiency or reductions in dispersal cost lead to overexploitation and the emergence of social inequality, with a minority of mobile, overexploitative agents and a majority of frugal, sedentary agents. Rapid social learning (impatience) and local information (myopia) further aggravate inequality and inefficiency. The model identifies key moderators of efficiency and equity but does not include or analyze punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how efficiency in spatial public goods–like environments depends on ecological and behavioral parameters such as harvesting efficiency, dispersal cost, consumer density, and information structure. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctioning, as these mechanisms are absent from the model. Therefore, while the findings are useful for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions and for understanding the role of mobility and social learning, the paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to the introduction of punishment. It is best used for baseline efficiency estimation and for understanding moderators of overexploitation and inequality in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Song, Q. Q., Li, Z. P., Fu, C. H., & Wang, L. S. (2011). Optional contributions have positive effects for volunteering public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing an option for low contributions in volunteering public goods games (VPG) increases the average payoff of the population (i.e., efficiency), compared to the standard VPG with only a single contribution level. This effect is observed in both well-mixed and spatially structured populations, and is especially pronounced when the multiplication factor (r) is above a threshold that depends weakly on group size. The mechanism is that the low-contribution option induces more players to cooperate, which in turn increases the number of high contributors as well, leading to higher overall payoffs. The results are based on theoretical analysis and extensive simulations of evolutionary dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in VPG-like public goods games, allowing optional (especially low) contributions can increase group efficiency compared to a single, higher contribution requirement. While the paper does not study punishment or sanctions, it is highly informative for predicting efficiency in games where the main design dimension is the contribution structure (e.g., all-or-nothing vs. continuous, or optional vs. required). The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency, introducing more flexible contribution options (even without punishment) can increase efficiency, especially when the MPCR is above a moderate threshold. However, the paper does not address the effects of punishment, so it is not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Yamamoto, T., Ito, H., Nii, M., Okabe, T., Morita, S., & Yoshimura, J. (2019). A single 'weight-lifting' game covers all kinds of games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper demonstrates mathematically that the weight-lifting game can represent all major two-player cooperation/defection games by adjusting three parameters: the incremental success probabilities from cooperation and the cost-to-benefit ratio. It provides a unified payoff matrix and phase diagrams showing how efficiency and cooperation depend on these parameters. The model is shown to encompass the standard public goods game as a special case when the success probability increases linearly with the number of cooperators. The paper does not consider punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how efficiency and group payoff outcomes in public-goods-like games depend on the underlying payoff structure, especially the cost/benefit ratio and the synergy of cooperation. However, it does not address punishment, sanctions, or any intervention mechanism, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for baseline modeling of efficiency in the absence of punishment, or for understanding how the structure of the game (e.g., synergy, cost/benefit) sets the stage for potential intervention effects.

- source: Quan, J., Zhang, X. M., Chen, W. M., & Wang, X. J. (2023). Cooperation dynamics in collective risk games with endogenous endowments. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a two-stage PGG-CRG model with endogenous endowments, higher perceived risk and lower required contribution ratios increase the proportion of the population that successfully avoids catastrophic loss (target achieved rate). The effect of the first-stage enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) is non-monotonic: when the required contribution ratio is small, higher enhancement factors help, but at moderate contribution ratios, a moderate enhancement factor is optimal. The model does not include or analyze punishment or sanction mechanisms, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or collective risk games, as no punishment or sanction mechanism is present in the model. However, it does provide informative context on how other game design parameters (risk, contribution ratio, enhancement factor) affect cooperation and group success in a threshold public goods/collective risk environment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency or cooperation rates in the absence of punishment.

- source: Ezoe, H., & Ikegawa, Y. (2013). Coexistence of mutualists and non-mutualists in a dual-lattice model. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that spatial structure allows for the persistence and coexistence of mutualists and non-mutualists in a dual-lattice model, even when mutualists pay a cost to benefit others. The parameter range for mutualist persistence expands as the magnitude of interspecific interaction increases. Asymmetry in basic reproduction rates between species can facilitate coexistence. The model does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms, and outcomes are not reported in terms of efficiency or group payoff, but rather in terms of population densities and coexistence patterns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms. It is relevant for understanding the effects of spatial structure and interspecific interaction on the persistence of cooperation, but does not inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment. The model and findings are adjacent to the prediction task, but not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Evolution of Public Cooperation in a Risky Society with Heterogeneous Assets. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that asset heterogeneity (a large gap between rich and poor) hinders cooperation, regardless of the temptation to defect. Increasing the value of individual assets or the risk of collective loss promotes cooperation. High thresholds and low risk can destroy cooperation, especially when asset inequality is high. The proportion of poor in the population also affects cooperation: when the poor are a small minority, they tend to free ride; when they are a majority, more contribute. The model does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff. However, it is informative about how asset heterogeneity, risk, and threshold affect cooperation rates in threshold public goods games with collective risk. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may provide contextual insight into how structural features (like risk and heterogeneity) affect cooperation in the absence of punishment.

- source: Shi, Y. D., Zhong, L. X., & Xu, W. J. (2013). Effects of group sensitivity on cooperation in N-person snowdrift game with dynamic grouping. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in an N-person snowdrift game where agents can adjust their preferred group size based on group sensitivity (a parameter reflecting emotional response to group outcomes), moderate group sensitivity leads to the highest levels of cooperation. Both the cost-to-benefit ratio and group sensitivity affect the evolution of preferred group size and cooperation frequency. There is a functional relationship between the frequency of cooperators and the average preferred group size. However, the paper does not include punishment, sanctions, or direct efficiency/payoff analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It is relevant for understanding how group size dynamics and emotional factors (group sensitivity) can affect cooperation rates, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Liu, Y. Y., Liu, L. J., Guo, R. Q., & Zhang, L. (2023). N-player repeated evolutionary trust game under government management. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a government-managed punishment and reward mechanism in a repeated N-player trust game can sustain trust and investment, leading to higher group payoffs. There is a critical threshold of punishment intensity: below it, untrustworthy behavior dominates and payoffs are low; above it, trust and investment are maintained, and payoffs increase. The model also shows that as punishment intensity increases further, the system can reach a stable coexistence of strategies with high trust and investment. The results are robust across different group sizes and parameter values, and are supported by both theoretical analysis and numerical simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated trust-game-like environments with group structure, introducing or increasing the intensity of punishment (and associated rewards for reporting) can substantially increase group efficiency/payoff, provided the punishment is above a critical threshold. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, so the qualitative prediction that punishment increases efficiency is likely to transfer to PGG-like settings, especially those with repeated interactions and third-party enforcement. The results are most informative for settings where punishment is implemented by an external authority and is contingent on reporting.

- source: Langlois, C. C., & Langlois, J. P. P. (2004). Provisions for noncompliance and treaty value: A game theoretic perspective. *International Studies Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with possible unintentional noncompliance (noise), the design of punishment schemes has a large effect on long-run efficiency (treaty value). Probabilistic trigger schemes, which are analogous to mutual punishment phases after a deviation, are self-punishing and reduce efficiency because both the perpetrator and the victim suffer during punishment. Contrite tit-for-tat (CTFT) schemes, which allow for redress (compensation to the victim) and delay retaliation (generosity), achieve much higher efficiency by minimizing the time spent in mutual defection and ensuring that only the guilty party is punished. The paper provides explicit formulas and comparative statics for how efficiency declines with noise under each scheme, and shows that CTFT dominates trigger schemes in most of the relevant parameter space.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the structure of the punishment scheme (especially whether punishment is self-punishing or allows for redress) is a critical moderator of the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated social dilemma games. If the punishment mechanism is costly to the punisher and involves mutual punishment phases (as in standard trigger schemes), efficiency is substantially reduced, especially as noise increases. If the punishment mechanism allows for redress (compensation to the victim) and avoids mutual punishment (as in CTFT), efficiency is much higher. The paper also shows that the speed of retaliation (trigger probability) and the expected duration of punishment phases are important: swifter, shorter punishments are more efficient, but only if they still deter defection. These insights should inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment based on the punishment technology, cost structure, and whether redress is possible.

- source: Chassang, S., & Zehnder, C. (2016). Rewards and punishments: Informal contracting through social preferences. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that informal punishment and reward schemes, when based on social preferences and ex post moral judgment, can partially or fully internalize externalities and improve efficiency, but the effect depends on the type of justice (intent-based vs. outcome-based), the quality of information, and the nature of the externality (positive or negative). Intent-based justice can achieve high efficiency with good monitoring but is less robust and can result in multiple equilibria, including inefficient ones. Outcome-based justice is more robust but less efficient, as it does not exploit side information and always incurs transfer costs. The model provides explicit formulas for the efficiency loss under each regime and characterizes when punishment will or will not be used.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical predictions about how the structure and effectiveness of punishment (and reward) mechanisms, the cost and technology of punishment, and the quality of information affect efficiency in environments with externalities. While not a direct PGG, the model is highly informative for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled, especially in settings where a principal or arbitrator determines sanctions based on observed outcomes and beliefs. The distinction between intent-based and outcome-based justice, and the explicit efficiency bounds derived, can inform predictions about the likely efficiency gains or limits from punishment in related experimental or field PGGs, especially when considering the role of monitoring precision, transfer costs, and the possibility of multiple equilibria.

- source: Nakao, K. (2009). CREATION OF SOCIAL ORDER IN ETHNIC CONFLICT. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of punishment mechanisms (peer monitoring, collective peer punishment, or ethnic conflict) can sustain higher group efficiency (expected payoffs) compared to no punishment, but only if in-group monitoring is sufficiently efficient and not too costly. When monitoring is inefficient or costly, or when noise (misinterpretation of actions) is high, punishment can reduce efficiency due to frequent erroneous punishment or high monitoring costs. The model provides comparative statics showing that efficiency is maximized with efficient peer monitoring and high tolerance, and that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the quality of in-group policing, the length of conflict, and the density of inter-group interactions. The model predicts that groups with high-quality in-group policing achieve higher efficiency and more stable cooperation, while groups with low-quality policing suffer more frequent and longer conflicts, reducing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated social dilemma games depends critically on the efficiency and cost of monitoring and punishment. If the game design allows for efficient, low-cost peer monitoring (low punishment cost, high punishment tech), enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to the control. If monitoring is costly or ineffective, punishment can reduce efficiency due to high costs or frequent erroneous punishment. The model also shows that the effect of punishment is moderated by noise (misinterpretation), group size, and the structure of interactions. For prediction, the paper suggests that enabling punishment will increase efficiency only when monitoring is efficient and not too costly, and that the marginal effect of punishment is lower or negative when monitoring is costly or noise is high. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, so findings should be transferred with caution.

- source: Yaman, A., Leibo, J. Z., Iacca, G., & Wan Lee, S. (2023). The emergence of division of labour through decentralized social sanctioning. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that decentralized social sanctioning, implemented as a matrix of role-based rules for reward and punishment, enables groups of self-interested agents to learn and maintain efficient divisions of labour. This leads to substantially higher average group payoff compared to groups without sanctioning, or with only altruistic or selfish learning. The effect is robust across different environmental conditions and is close to the theoretical upper bound. Complexity regularization can promote the evolution of simpler, convergent social norms without sacrificing group payoff. The model shows that sanctioning transforms the incentive structure, making it individually rational to take on critical but otherwise unrewarding roles, thus solving the social dilemma.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and computational evidence that enabling decentralized social sanctioning (punishment and/or reward) in public-goods-like environments can substantially increase group efficiency, especially when critical roles are otherwise unrewarding. The effect is robust across a range of environmental parameters and is close to the optimal achievable by global optimization. The model is not a direct PGG but is structurally adjacent, with payoff interdependence and social dilemmas. The findings support the prediction that, all else equal, enabling punishment (and/or reward) will increase efficiency relative to a control with sanctioning disabled, especially in settings where division of labour is required. The model also highlights the importance of norm complexity and the potential for simple sanctioning rules to be highly effective.

- source: Balmaceda, F., & Escobar, J. F. (2017). Trust in cohesive communities. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency and welfare effects of punishment in repeated trust games depend critically on the structure of the social network and the speed of information flow. When information about defections spreads only to direct neighbors (slow information), cohesive networks (complete components) are optimal for sustaining cooperation and maximizing welfare, as they allow for coordinated punishment. When information spreads rapidly to all direct and indirect connections (fast information), minimally connected networks (e.g., trees) are optimal, as they maximize the number of players who can punish a defection. The paper also shows that the convexity or concavity of the welfare function determines whether stars or equally sized components maximize welfare. The analysis provides explicit characterizations of equilibrium payoffs and welfare under different network and monitoring regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how network structure and information transmission technology moderate the efficiency gains from punishment in repeated trust-like games. It shows that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not uniform but depends on whether the network allows for coordinated punishment and how quickly information about defections spreads. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that game design dimensions related to network structure, monitoring technology, and information flow are critical moderators of punishment's efficiency effects. However, the model is not a standard public goods game and does not provide empirical effect sizes, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution and only in settings structurally similar to the model.

- source: Stahl, D. O. (2011). Cooperation in the sporadically repeated prisoners' dilemma via reputation mechanisms. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in the SRPD, only a small subset of reputation mechanisms can sustain cooperation and achieve efficient outcomes. Specifically, mechanisms that distinguish between justified punishment and defection, and allow for penitence, can support equilibria where the long-run group payoff matches the fully cooperative outcome. The analysis shows that, with these mechanisms and high enough continuation probability, the population evolves toward full cooperation and efficiency. In contrast, most reputation mechanisms result in universal defection and zero efficiency. The paper also extends the analysis to clan-based (group) settings, showing that the efficiency result generalizes when within-group matching is sufficiently likely.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the structure of the punishment/reputation mechanism is a critical moderator of the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. If the punishment mechanism is designed to avoid penalizing justified punishers and allows for penitence, and if the continuation probability is high, then enabling punishment can move efficiency from zero (universal defection) to the full-cooperation benchmark. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the details of the punishment/reputation update rule and the effective 'delta' (continuation probability) are crucial for forecasting the efficiency impact of punishment. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, anonymous-matching games with public reputations, but the logic may transfer to PGG-like settings with similar reputation mechanisms.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, X. K., Wang, X. J., Yang, J. B., Wu, K. B., & Dai, Z. L. (2019). Withhold-judgment and punishment promote cooperation in indirect reciprocity under incomplete information. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of punishment in indirect reciprocity models with incomplete information generally increases cooperation rates, but its effect on efficiency (average payoff) depends on the social norm and parameter settings. In some cases, especially when punishment is frequent and costly, the net effect on social welfare is negative, even as cooperation rises. Social norms that allow observers to withhold judgment (not update reputation) in ambiguous cases are more robust, especially under private reputation and noisy/incomplete information, and can maintain both high cooperation and high average payoff. The effect of punishment is thus context-dependent: it can either improve or harm efficiency depending on the interplay of punishment cost, observation probability, and the social norm in use.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments is not universally positive: while punishment reliably increases cooperation, it can reduce efficiency if the cost of punishment is high or if punishment is overused. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related (donation game with indirect reciprocity and group-level outcomes). The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, one must consider not only the presence of punishment but also its cost, the effectiveness of punishment, the information structure (public vs private reputation), and the social norm governing reputation updates. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not always a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment), as punishment can sometimes lower group welfare despite increasing cooperation.

- source: Honjo, K., & Kubo, T. (2020). Social Dilemmas in Nature-Based Tourism Depend on Social Value Orientations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of outcomes in a two-player common-pool resource game depends critically on the social value orientations (SVOs) of the players. When both players are competitive, the Nash equilibrium is often Pareto-inefficient (i.e., group efficiency is low), and this inefficiency is robust to the introduction of fair punishment. In fact, punishment can increase the parameter space in which inefficiency occurs for competitive players. When at least one player is individualistic or prosocial, the Nash equilibrium is almost always Pareto efficient (i.e., high group efficiency). The model demonstrates that punishment does not always improve efficiency and can backfire in certain social contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games is highly sensitive to the social preferences of the players. In environments where players are competitive (i.e., care about outperforming others), introducing fair punishment can worsen efficiency, increasing the likelihood of Pareto-inefficient outcomes. In contrast, with individualistic or prosocial players, efficiency is generally high even without punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee improved efficiency and may even reduce it in competitive settings, especially in small, binary-choice, one-shot games. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, single-round, all-or-nothing, common-pool resource dilemmas, but the underlying logic may transfer to similar public goods environments.

- source: Camera, G., & Gioffré, A. (2014). A tractable analysis of contagious equilibria. *Journal of Mathematical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that contagious punishment (grim trigger strategies) in repeated random-matching social dilemmas can sustain full cooperation (i.e., maximal efficiency) if players are sufficiently patient (high discount factor), the cost of cooperation is not too high, and monitoring is sufficiently broad. The authors derive closed-form expressions for continuation payoffs and provide exact bounds on the discount factor and the cost of cooperation required to make cooperation self-enforcing. They show that increasing the breadth of monitoring (i.e., allowing players to observe more actions outside their own match) makes it easier to sustain cooperation, lowering the required discount factor and cost of cooperation. Conversely, if defection is not absorbing (i.e., if players can revert to cooperation), sustaining cooperation becomes harder. The results are general for random-matching repeated games with contagious punishment, and the framework is intended to inform both theory and laboratory design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the presence and structure of punishment (specifically, contagious, unforgiving punishment) can enable high efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, even with random matching and anonymity. The key moderators are the discount factor, the cost of cooperation, and the breadth of monitoring (how many others' actions are observed). The results suggest that, in environments with strong punishment and sufficient monitoring, enabling punishment should increase efficiency relative to control (no punishment), provided the other parameters (patience, cost) are favorable. The paper also provides explicit formulas for the parameter thresholds needed to sustain cooperation, which could be used to predict when punishment will be effective in raising efficiency. However, the results are theoretical and apply to repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-type games, not public goods games per se, though the logic is closely related.

- source: Melkonyan, T., Zeitoun, H., & Chater, N. (2022). The cognitive foundations of tacit commitments: A virtual bargaining model of dynamic interactions. *Journal of Mathematical Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in dynamic games without external enforcement, the possibility of tacit commitment to punishment (as formalized by the virtual bargaining equilibrium) enables self-interested agents to achieve efficient, mutually beneficial outcomes that are not predicted by standard subgame perfect equilibrium. In games like the finitely repeated Prisoner's Dilemma, Centipede, and repeated Bertrand competition, VBE supports cooperation, collusion, or turn-taking, enforced by credible threats of punishment (even if costly). This leads to higher group payoffs and efficiency compared to the standard prediction. However, if players cannot commit to future punishment (SVBE), the threat is not credible and efficiency collapses to the Nash/subgame perfect level.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling credible punishment (even if costly to the punisher) can substantially increase group efficiency in repeated or sequential social dilemma games, relative to control conditions where punishment is not possible or not credible. The model is not a direct PGG or lab experiment, but the logic applies to adjacent environments (e.g., repeated PD, Centipede, repeated Bertrand) that share key features with public goods games. The findings suggest that, when game design allows for credible commitment to punishment, efficiency is likely to be much higher than in control games without such commitment. The effect is robust to the absence of external enforcement or non-standard preferences. However, the model assumes agents reason as virtual bargainers; if they do not, or if commitment is not possible, efficiency gains disappear. The paper is most informative for settings where punishment is possible, costly, and can be credibly pre-committed.

- source: Thomadsen, R., & Bhardwaj, P. (2011). Cooperation in Games with Forgetfulness. *Management Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of forgetfulness on efficiency in repeated games with punishment is fundamentally ambiguous: it can either reduce or increase the set of discount factors and game parameters under which full cooperation (and thus maximum efficiency) is sustainable. When the stage game is such that the cooperative action always yields nonnegative payoffs, forgetfulness weakens punishment and makes cooperation harder to sustain, reducing efficiency. However, when the stage game allows for the possibility that a forgetful player can be punished below the minimax, forgetfulness can make severe punishments credible and thus expand the range of parameters supporting cooperation, increasing efficiency. The paper provides explicit formulas and graphical illustrations for these effects in the repeated prisoner's dilemma and a franchising effort game. The results are theoretical and depend on the structure of the stage game and the probability of forgetfulness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games is not monotonic and can be moderated by cognitive or institutional features like forgetfulness. In environments where punishment is only as severe as the stage minimax, introducing forgetfulness will generally reduce the efficiency gains from punishment. However, in environments where forgetfulness allows for below-minimax punishments, punishment can become more effective at sustaining cooperation, potentially increasing efficiency beyond what is possible with perfect recall. For prediction tasks, this means that the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined solely from standard game design dimensions; one must also consider whether the environment allows for forgetfulness and whether the stage game payoffs permit below-minimax punishments. The paper does not provide empirical estimates but gives clear theoretical boundaries and mechanisms.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2017). A remarkable effect of the combination of probabilistic peerpunishment and coevolutionary mechanism on the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing probabilistic peer-punishment based on payoff differences, together with a coevolutionary mechanism allowing players to break and form connections, leads to a substantial increase in the average payoff of all players (i.e., group efficiency) in evolutionary prisoner's dilemma games on networks. This effect is especially pronounced in random and scale-free networks and for intermediate values of the punishment coefficient. The combination of these mechanisms expands the parameter range (punishment coefficient r) over which high cooperation and high average payoff are sustained, compared to models with only one or neither mechanism. The effect is less pronounced or absent in regular networks with high average degree.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in evolutionary networked prisoner's dilemma games, enabling a punishment mechanism (specifically, probabilistic peer-punishment based on payoff differences) increases group efficiency (average payoff), especially when combined with a coevolutionary mechanism for network rewiring. The effect is robust across a range of network topologies and average degrees, but is most pronounced in random and scale-free networks and for intermediate punishment cost/effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in similar networked games is likely to increase efficiency, particularly when players can also adjust their social ties. However, the results are from a simulation model, not a human experiment, and the game is a prisoner's dilemma on a network, not a standard public goods game.

- source: Libois, F. (2022). Success and failure of communities managing natural resources: Static and dynamic inefficiencies. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model shows that introducing sanctions (punishment) can increase efficiency in CPR games by enabling the enforcement of rules that prevent over-extraction (static inefficiency) and/or promote conservation (dynamic inefficiency). The effect of punishment on efficiency depends on the strength of the sanctioning mechanism relative to the incentives to deviate from cooperation. When sanctions are strong enough, the first-best (fully efficient) outcome can be achieved. With limited sanctions, only partial improvements are possible, and sometimes increasing the resource's value or growth rate can make enforcement harder, reducing efficiency. The model also shows that the number of players, extraction cost, and inequality among players all moderate the effect of sanctions on efficiency. In particular, more players or greater inequality can make conservation harder to enforce, while higher extraction costs can make it easier. The model provides explicit formulas and comparative statics for these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment (sanctions) on efficiency in CPR-like games. It shows that the effect of punishment depends on the interplay between the sanctioning technology (maximum enforceable sanction), the number of players, the resource's growth rate, extraction cost, and initial value. If sanctions are strong enough relative to the incentives to deviate, enabling rules can be enforced and efficiency increases. If not, efficiency may not improve, or may even decrease if resource value/growth increases incentives to defect. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, and the logic of how punishment affects efficiency is highly transferable. The paper is especially informative for settings where the punishment mechanism is exogenous and bounded, and where resource dynamics (growth, conservation) are important. For prediction, the model suggests that the effect of enabling punishment depends on whether the sanctioning parameters cross key thresholds relative to the game's other parameters.

- source: Mondal, S. S., Pathak, M., & Chakraborty, S. (2022). Reward versus punishment: averting the tragedy of the commons in eco-evolutionary dynamics. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward can prevent the tragedy of the commons in a dynamic resource game, but their effects on efficiency differ. Sufficiently strong punishment leads to a stable equilibrium with all cooperators and a fully replete resource, achieving the social optimum (maximum possible group payoff). In contrast, even with strong reward, some defectors persist, so the group does not reach the maximum efficiency. The model also shows that the effect of punishment or reward depends on the initial state and the intensity of the intervention, and that bistability (multiple possible long-run outcomes) can occur. The analysis is theoretical, using replicator dynamics and stability analysis, and is not based on empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in dynamic resource games with feedback (and by extension, in public-goods-like environments), enabling punishment (with sufficient intensity) can increase group efficiency and achieve the social optimum, while enabling reward alone cannot fully eliminate inefficiency due to persistent defectors. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the game is a prisoner's dilemma with resource feedback, and where punishment or reward is implemented as a direct modification of payoffs. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment should be expected to increase efficiency more than enabling reward, especially when the goal is to reach the maximum possible group payoff. However, the model is theoretical and does not address practical implementation details or empirical constraints.

- source: Voelkl, B. (2015). The evolution of generalized reciprocity in social interaction networks. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: Theoretical analysis demonstrates that introducing an effective penalty for unfaithfulness (selling outside the cooperative) leads to stable, efficient cooperative outcomes where all members sell to the cooperative. If the penalty is limited (not strong enough), full cooperation is unstable. When the penalty is strong (exceeds the extra profit from defection), the system converges to full cooperation, maximizing group payoff. Redistribution of penalties among faithful members further strengthens this effect, leading to a unique, stable Nash equilibrium with high efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions under which punishment stabilizes cooperation and maximizes group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in public-goods-like settings with continuous contribution and the possibility of punishment, increasing the effectiveness and/or strength of punishment increases group efficiency and stabilizes cooperation. The results are directly relevant for predicting that enabling or strengthening punishment will increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is strong enough to outweigh the gains from defection. The model also highlights that redistributing penalties among cooperators can further enhance efficiency and stability. However, the results are derived from a stylized, theoretical model rather than empirical data, and the setting is a marketing cooperative with continuous contribution rather than a canonical PGG, though the structure is closely related.

- source: Duéñez-Guzmán, E. A., & Sadedin, S. (2012). Evolving Righteousness in a Corrupt World. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a small benefit to punishers (such as status or a payment from cooperators) destabilizes corrupt equilibria and enables the evolution and stability of 'righteousness'—a state where cooperative punishers dominate and both corruption and defection are suppressed. Righteousness is associated with higher collective payoffs (efficiency/wellbeing) than either corruption or defection. The model demonstrates that egalitarian and harsh punishment (i.e., similar and sufficiently high penalties for defectors and corrupt punishers) are key to achieving righteousness. Power inequalities that favor corrupt punishers stabilize corruption, but removing or reducing these inequalities (and/or providing small benefits to punishers) can shift the population to righteousness. Once established, righteousness is highly stable and resistant to invasion by defectors or corrupt punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public-goods-like environments, enabling punishment (especially when punishers receive even small additional benefits) can increase group efficiency/wellbeing compared to control conditions without punishment or with only corrupt punishment. The model suggests that the design dimensions most relevant for maximizing efficiency gains from punishment are: (1) ensuring punishers are not disadvantaged relative to cooperators (e.g., via small payments or status), (2) making punishment of defectors and corrupt punishers sufficiently harsh, and (3) minimizing power inequalities that allow corrupt punishers to avoid punishment. While the model is not an exact PGG, its structure and outcomes are highly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games, especially regarding the role of corruption, punishment severity, and benefits to punishers.

- source: Lim, I. S., & Capraro, V. (2022). A Synergy of Institutional Incentives and Networked Structures in Evolutionary Game Dynamics of Multiagent Systems. *Ieee Transactions on Circuits and Systems Ii-express Briefs*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment and network structure can interact synergistically to promote prosocial behavior and increase group efficiency (mean payoff) in a trust game. Specifically, the combination of even modest punishment and network structure can achieve full cooperation and maximal efficiency in parameter regions where neither mechanism alone would suffice. The required punishment level to achieve full cooperation is lower in structured populations than in well-mixed populations. The mean payoff is maximized at the threshold punishment level that induces full cooperation; increasing punishment beyond this point does not further increase efficiency and may reduce it due to the cost of maintaining the incentive system. The paper provides explicit analytical boundaries for these effects as a function of the trust return (r), network degree (k), and punishment size (p).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a networked trust game (and by extension, in adjacent public-goods-like games) will increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is not too costly and the network structure is not too sparse. The results suggest that the efficiency gain from punishment is greater in structured populations than in well-mixed ones, and that the combination of network structure and punishment can achieve high efficiency at lower punishment levels than punishment alone. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the network structure (degree), the effectiveness and cost of punishment, and the underlying return to cooperation. The findings are most directly applicable to trust games but are likely to transfer to public goods games with similar strategic structure.

- source: Gámez, M., López, I., Rodríguez, C., Varga, Z., & Garay, J. (2018). Game-theoretical model for marketing cooperative in fisheries. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that effective punishment (penalty parameter β > 1) stabilizes full cooperation in a marketing cooperative, leading to the group achieving the maximum possible profit (i.e., efficiency). When punishment is limited (β = 1), cooperation is unstable and members tend to defect, reducing group profit. The model is dynamic, with payoffs and strategies evolving over time, but the key result is that sufficiently strong punishment ensures convergence to the efficient outcome. The findings are demonstrated both analytically and through simulation examples.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling sufficiently strong punishment in a public-goods-like environment (here, a marketing cooperative) will increase efficiency, moving the group toward the maximum possible collective payoff. The model is not a standard PGG but is structurally similar: members choose how much to contribute to the group (by selling through the cooperative), and the group can punish defectors. The explicit analysis of payoff functions and the demonstration that efficiency is achieved only when punishment is strong enough (β > 1) are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in settings where the punishment technology is effective and the cost/impact ratio is favorable. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Wang, X. L., Wu, J., Shu, G., & Li, Y. (2014). Punishment Based on Public Benefit Fund Significantly Promotes Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment funded by a public benefit fund (taxes on high earners) significantly increases both cooperation and average payoff (efficiency) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. Slight punishment (low intensity, broad coverage) is more effective than severe punishment (high intensity, narrow coverage) in raising average payoff. The effect of punishment is stronger when the temptation to defect is higher. The results are robust across square lattice and small world network topologies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (with costs funded by a public benefit fund) increases group efficiency (average payoff) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game, compared to no punishment. The effect is stronger for slight punishment (low intensity, broad coverage) than for severe punishment (high intensity, narrow coverage). The findings are most directly relevant to spatial PD games with local interactions, not standard PGGs, but the efficiency outcome is directly reported. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially with broad coverage and moderate intensity) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, especially as the temptation to defect increases.

- source: Nakamaru, M., Shimura, H., Kitakaji, Y., & Ohnuma, S. (2018). The effect of sanctions on the evolution of cooperation in linear division of labor. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing sanctions—especially the producer responsibility system—promotes the evolution of cooperation and increases group-level efficiency in a linear division of labor game. The actor responsibility system only promotes cooperation if monitoring and detection are sufficiently effective, which is often not the case in real-world settings. The producer responsibility system, which sanctions the generator if the manifest is not returned, is robust to low monitoring probabilities and is more effective at promoting cooperation and reducing illegal dumping. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation is stable and shows that the three-role model captures more realistic dynamics than simpler two-role models. The findings are supported by both analytical results and numerical simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that introducing a well-designed punishment system (especially one that does not rely on costly monitoring, like the producer responsibility system) will increase group efficiency in public-goods-like environments with linear division of labor. The model is adjacent to standard PGGs but is directly relevant for settings with sequential roles and externalities. The results suggest that, for games with similar structure, enabling punishment (especially with high effectiveness and low monitoring requirements) should be predicted to increase efficiency relative to control. The paper also highlights the importance of the effectiveness of monitoring and the structure of the sanction system as moderators. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide direct empirical estimates of effect sizes.

- source: Yu, T. K., Chen, S. H., & Li, H. G. (2016). Social norms, costly punishment and the evolution of cooperation. *Journal of Economic Interaction and Coordination*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that allowing or encouraging costly punishment in a donor-recipient game increases both the size of the basin of attraction for the cooperative evolutionary stable state (CESS) and the speed at which a society converges to this high-payoff state. Under parameter settings where punishment is not too costly, the presence of punishment options increases the probability that a society will escape the non-cooperative trap and reach high efficiency. The effect is stronger when the social norm actively encourages punishment. However, in the long run, after full cooperation is achieved, continued costly punishment can reduce efficiency due to unnecessary punishment costs. The findings are based on mathematical modeling and numerical simulations, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated social dilemma environments with reputation and norm dynamics, enabling costly punishment increases the likelihood and speed of reaching high-efficiency cooperative states, especially when the social norm encourages punishment. The effect is most pronounced in 'developing' societies or initial conditions with many defectors. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment (especially with supportive norms) will generally increase efficiency relative to control, unless punishment is extremely costly. However, in fully cooperative steady states, punishment may reduce efficiency due to its cost. The model is not a standard PGG but is structurally similar, so findings are likely transferable to PGG-like settings with reputation and norm evolution.

- source: Ito, H., & Yoshimura, J. (2015). Social penalty promotes cooperation in a cooperative society. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a social penalty (punishment) for non-cooperative actions in already-cooperative societies universally increases cooperation and group welfare in theoretical models of the hawk-dove and prisoner's dilemma games. The penalty reduces the payoff of non-cooperators, shifting the equilibrium toward more cooperation. The effect is robust to the size of the penalty: larger penalties can lead to full cooperation. The results are generalized to non-cooperative games, with the claim that social penalties always promote cooperation and never reduce it. The paper also discusses the implications for real-world legal systems and public goods provision, but does not model or simulate public goods games directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that introducing or increasing punishment for non-cooperation will increase group efficiency and average payoff in non-cooperative games, including those adjacent to public goods games. The results are derived analytically and show that the effect of punishment is always non-negative for cooperation and group welfare. However, the models are not public goods games per se, and do not address multi-player, repeated, or more complex institutional features. The findings are most directly applicable to settings where punishment is externally imposed and not subject to strategic choice by players. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control, especially as punishment becomes more severe or certain.

- source: Brandt, U. S., & Svendsen, G. T. (2019). How robust is the welfare state when facing open borders? An evolutionary game-theoretic model. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The model shows that costly punishment (by Willing Punishers) is essential for maintaining high average welfare in a society vulnerable to free riding. If the proportion of punishers falls below a threshold, defectors proliferate and average welfare collapses. Timely recognition of the threat and sufficient support (redistributive rewards) for punishers can reverse this trend and restore high welfare. The model identifies threshold effects: there is a minimum level of support for punishment required to sustain cooperation and high efficiency. Delays in shifting support to punishers or insufficient rewards for them lead to welfare loss or collapse. The model also shows cyclical dynamics, with periods of high and low welfare depending on the political narrative and group composition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence and effectiveness of costly punishment mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency in public-goods-like environments. The model suggests that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, but only if punishment is sufficiently incentivized and timely. The effect is not automatic: if support for punishers is too low or delayed, efficiency gains are not realized and may even be reversed. The findings support the prediction that punishment will increase efficiency when the game design ensures adequate support for punishers and rapid response to defection. The model also highlights the importance of migration/inflow of defectors and the political/institutional context (ingroup favouritism) as moderators. However, as a theoretical model, it does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct mapping to specific experimental game parameters.

- source: Barron, D., & Guo, Y. N. (2021). THE USE AND MISUSE OF COORDINATED PUNISHMENTS. *Quarterly Journal of Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: chat, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in relational/trust games with coordinated punishment, the possibility of extortion (i.e., agents threatening to trigger punishment unless paid, regardless of their effort) can completely undermine cooperation and efficiency. In the baseline model with extortion, equilibrium effort and surplus are zero. However, if institutions can tie the ability to trigger punishment (leverage) to actual effort—such as through public signals of effort or strong dyadic relationships—then positive effort and efficiency can be restored, though often at the cost of rents to agents or the need for strong relationships. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore not universally positive: it depends critically on whether misuse/extortion is possible and whether institutional features can prevent it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated cooperation games is highly sensitive to the possibility of extortion and the institutional context. If punishment can be misused (e.g., via action-contingent threats), enabling punishment may reduce efficiency to zero. However, if the game design includes mechanisms that tie punishment power to actual cooperative behavior (e.g., public effort signals, strong dyadic relationships), punishment can support high efficiency. For prediction, this means that the presence or absence of extortion opportunities, and the presence of institutional safeguards, are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency. Game design dimensions that affect the ability to make credible threats, the observability of effort, and the structure of relationships are especially important.

- source: Heller, W. B., & Sieberg, K. K. (2010). Honor among thieves: Cooperation as a strategic response to functional unpleasantness. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and high group efficiency can emerge and persist in populations where all individuals are fundamentally self-interested and prefer to cheat, as long as some are willing to punish cheaters and some are strategic enough to cooperate when the risk of being punished is high. The presence of 'Strategic Unpleasant' types—who punish cheaters and cooperate only to avoid punishment—can lead to equilibria where most or all players cooperate, resulting in high average payoffs (efficiency). The model provides explicit payoff matrices and equilibrium conditions showing that, under reasonable parameter values (punishment not too costly, punishment more aversive than being cheated, etc.), punishment enables efficient outcomes. The analysis also shows that if punishment is too costly or ineffective, cooperation and efficiency break down.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment will increase group efficiency, provided that punishment is not excessively costly and is sufficiently effective to deter cheating. The model clarifies that the efficiency gain from punishment depends on the cost/impact structure of punishment and the strategic response of players. If the control (no-punishment) game is dominated by cheaters (low efficiency), introducing punishment—even by self-interested types—can shift the equilibrium to high efficiency. The paper's explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium conditions can be used to inform quantitative predictions, especially regarding the importance of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the strategic incentives for cooperation.

- source: Vincent, J. R. (2007). Spatial dynamics, social norms, and the opportunity of the commons. *Ecological Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that economic incentives—specifically, the use of sanctions (punishments for non-cooperation and rewards for cooperation)—are critical for achieving efficient and sustainable management of common pool resources. Theoretical models (e.g., repeated games, evolutionary game theory) and empirical case studies (e.g., Ostrom 1990) show that monitoring and enforcement mechanisms enable groups to avoid the tragedy of the commons, leading to higher group profits and resource stocks. The author is skeptical that social norms alone, without sanctions, can achieve similar efficiency, especially when rapid or large-scale behavioral change is needed. The paper also notes that public goods (as opposed to common pool resources) face similar free-rider problems and require external enforcement (e.g., taxes, quotas) to achieve efficient provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment or sanctions in public-goods-game-like environments will increase efficiency, group payoff, and resource sustainability, especially when compared to environments without such mechanisms. The presence of monitoring and enforcement is highlighted as a key moderator: systems with sanctions are much more likely to achieve efficient outcomes than those relying on norms alone. The paper does not provide quantitative estimates but offers strong theoretical and qualitative support for the positive effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment/sanctions should be coded as a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially in settings analogous to common pool resources.

- source: Podobnik, B., Gabor, A. M., & Kirbis, I. S. (2019). Scale-free growth of human society based on cooperation and altruistic punishment. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that the system's growth rate (interpreted as efficiency or collective payoff) increases monotonically with the level of cooperation, which itself increases with the level of punishment. There is a threshold: if punishment is too high, the system can collapse (negative growth), but within reasonable bounds, higher punishment leads to more cooperation and higher efficiency. Empirical data suggest that less corrupt (more cooperative) countries tend to have higher fines, supporting the model's prediction that higher punishment is associated with higher cooperation. Survey data indicate the perceived fine needed to induce compliance, but do not directly measure payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public-goods-like environments, enabling or increasing punishment increases efficiency (system growth rate), up to a point. The model is not a direct PGG but is closely related, and the main mechanism—punishment increasing cooperation, which increases collective payoff—transfers to PGG settings. The paper does not provide empirical estimates of effect size, but the qualitative direction is clear: punishment increases efficiency unless set excessively high. The model also highlights that the effect of punishment depends on the baseline level of cooperation and the distribution of punishment sensitivity in the population.

- source: Wei, X., Xu, P., Du, S. T., Yan, G. H., & Pei, H. Y. (2021). Reputational preference-based payoff punishment promotes cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a reputational preference-based payoff punishment mechanism in spatial social dilemmas (PDG and SDG) significantly increases the fraction of cooperators. At low punishment intensity, both cooperators and defectors may have low or negative average payoffs due to the cost of punishment, but as punishment intensity increases, cooperators' average payoff and fitness rise, and defectors' payoffs drop to zero. At high punishment intensity, the system can reach full cooperation with high average payoffs for cooperators. The effect is more pronounced in the snowdrift game, where coexistence of cooperators and defectors persists over a wider parameter range. The study does not directly report group-level efficiency, but the increase in average payoffs for cooperators at high punishment intensity suggests a positive effect on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical/simulation evidence that introducing a costly, reputation-based punishment mechanism in spatial social dilemmas increases cooperation rates and, at sufficient punishment intensity, increases average payoffs for cooperators, leading to higher group efficiency. The effect is robust across both prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games, with stronger effects in the latter. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment (especially with high effectiveness and shared cost) will increase efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, particularly in spatially structured games. However, the results are based on simulation, not empirical data, and do not use the public goods game directly, so transfer to PGG-like environments should be cautious but is likely informative.

- source: Gao, S. P., & Liang, J. L. (2020). Cooperation under institutional incentives with perfect and imperfect observation. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Institutional punishment (and reward), funded by a tax on positive payoffs, can promote the evolutionary emergence of cooperation in a well-mixed prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness of punishment increases with higher tax rates, greater benefit of cooperation, and stronger incentive effectiveness. There is a threshold tax rate above which cooperation is favored by natural selection. Imperfect monitoring (i.e., noise in identifying defectors) reduces the effectiveness of punishment and increases the required tax rate for cooperation to emerge. The model provides analytical results for average payoffs and fixation probabilities, but does not directly report group efficiency as a fraction of the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that institutional punishment (with costs funded by a tax on payoffs) can increase the evolutionary success of cooperation, especially when monitoring is perfect, the tax rate is high, and incentives are strong. While it does not report group efficiency directly, the increase in fixation probability of cooperation and average payoffs for cooperators suggest that enabling punishment in similar game designs would increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment, particularly under high tax rates and effective monitoring. The model is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a prisoner's dilemma structure, so predictions should be transferred with caution.
