The findings are most informative for settings with institutional, payoff-funded punishment and well-mixed populations.

- source: Wang, J. F., & Guo, J. L. (2019). A synergy of punishment and extortion in cooperation dilemmas driven by the leader. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a networked donation game, a single high-degree leader node using a combined punishment and extortion strategy can induce full cooperation in the population with probability close to 1, regardless of initial conditions or the presence of other strategies. This combined strategy yields the highest group and leader payoffs, and is robust to variations in the temptation to defect and the set of available strategies. Pure punishment or pure extortion by the leader is less effective. The results suggest a strong synergy between punishment and extortion in promoting cooperation and maximizing group welfare in networked social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in networked prisoner's dilemma/donation games, the presence of a punishment mechanism—especially when combined with extortion and wielded by a central leader—can dramatically increase group payoff and the probability of full cooperation. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, the findings are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments with network structure and leader influence. The results suggest that enabling punishment (especially in combination with other mechanisms) can shift efficiency from low to near-maximal, even when baseline cooperation is low. However, the effect depends on the presence of influential nodes and may not generalize to well-mixed or non-networked settings.

- source: Shen, C., Song, Z., Shi, L., Tanimoto, J., & Wang, Z. (2025). Exit options sustain altruistic punishment and decrease the second-order free-riders, but it is not a panacea. *Science China-information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that adding an exit option to a prisoner's dilemma with altruistic punishment changes the evolutionary dynamics. In well-mixed populations, exiters replace defectors as the exit payoff increases, but altruistic punishers do not become dominant. In networked populations, exiters can support the coexistence of altruistic punishers, defectors, and exiters via cyclic dominance, but this effect is unstable and depends on the exit payoff. Sometimes, non-punishing cooperators replace altruistic punishers, and the system can exhibit bi-stability between these outcomes. The effect of exiters on cooperation and punishment is nuanced and context-dependent. The paper does not provide direct predictions or measurements of group efficiency or welfare, but the results suggest that exit options can sometimes support, but also sometimes undermine, the mechanisms that sustain cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how the presence and incentive level of an exit option (voluntary participation) can affect the evolutionary stability of punishment and cooperation in social dilemmas. While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, it suggests that exit options can destabilize defection and, under some conditions in networked populations, support the coexistence of altruistic punishment. However, the effect is highly sensitive to the exit payoff and population structure, and exiters can also undermine punishment by enabling non-punishing cooperators to dominate. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of an exit option and its incentive level are important moderators of punishment's effect, especially in structured populations, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., Nie, S. L., & Wang, L. (2015). Promote or hinder? The role of punishment in the emergence of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation depends critically on the pattern of punishment and the presence of anti-social punishment. Centralized (pool) pro-social punishment is more effective than decentralized (peer) punishment at promoting cooperation. When anti-social punishment is possible and both pro- and anti-social punishments are in the same pattern, the positive effect of punishment is largely neutralized. However, if pro-social punishment is centralized and anti-social punishment is decentralized, cooperation can still be promoted. The results are derived from theoretical evolutionary game models and focus on the stationary abundance of strategies, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative theoretical evidence about how punishment design (centralized vs decentralized, presence of anti-social punishment) can affect the evolutionary success of cooperation in social dilemmas. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that centralized punishment mechanisms are more robust to anti-social punishment and more likely to sustain cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that games with centralized punishment (pool) are more likely to see positive effects of punishment on efficiency, especially when anti-social punishment is possible. However, the results are based on evolutionary dynamics and strategy abundance, not direct efficiency measures, and the model is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2015). Emergence of parochial altruism in well-mixed populations. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that parochial altruism (in-group cooperation, out-group punishment) is favored by selection under certain parameter regimes, especially when the benefit of cooperation is high relative to its cost, and when the effectiveness of punishment (fine imposed) exceeds its cost. Intergroup conflict (punishment) can promote the evolution of in-group cooperation, and vice versa. High mutation rates are detrimental to the evolution of parochial altruism. Larger populations and intermediate selection strengths can also favor parochial altruism. The model provides analytical conditions for when parochial altruism is favored, but does not directly report on group efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, by showing that punishment (when effective and not too costly) can promote the evolutionary success of parochial altruism (in-group cooperation and out-group punishment). However, the model is not a standard public goods game, but rather a two-group model with prisoner's dilemma for in-group and a punishment game for out-group interactions. The main outcomes are evolutionary frequencies of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under certain conditions, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency changes in standard PGGs. The findings are most relevant for understanding the parameter regimes where punishment is likely to support cooperative strategies, which may inform priors about efficiency effects in related experimental designs.

- source: Zu, J. J., Xu, F. X., Jin, T., & Xiang, W. (2022). Reward and Punishment Mechanism with weighting enhances cooperation in evolutionary games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that the introduction of a reward and punishment mechanism with weighting (RPMW) increases the frequency of cooperation in evolutionary games (PDG, SDG, SHG), both in well-mixed and lattice populations. The effect is stronger with higher intensity (alpha) and a higher proportion of supervisors (beta). The combination of reward and punishment is more effective than either alone in promoting cooperation. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation frequency, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment (and reward) mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma games, and that the effect is stronger with higher punishment/reward intensity and more supervisors. However, because the outcomes are reported as cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that, in similar game designs, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The specific game structure is not a public goods game but rather two-player matrix games (PDG, SDG, SHG), so transfer to PGG-like environments should be cautious.

- source: Schlaepfer, A. (2018). The emergence and selection of reputation systems that drive cooperative behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a population of initially non-cooperative individuals, a reputation system based on punishment is likely to emerge first and drive the initial evolution of cooperation. However, once cooperation is established, it is mainly sustained by a reputation system based on cooperative actions (social image). The model shows that cooperation rates are highest when both punishment-based and cooperation-based reputation systems are available. The emergence and stability of these systems depend on the costs of information acquisition (gossip) and the relevance of active gossip channels. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but focuses on cooperation rates and the use of reputation systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-based reputation systems can help establish cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, especially when information about others' behavior is costly to acquire. However, it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a standard public goods game (it uses a dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage). The findings suggest that punishment may be most important for the initial emergence of cooperation, but less so for its maintenance. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting the role of punishment-based reputation in overcoming initial defection.

- source: Wang, C. Y., & Cui, W. F. (2022). Supervision for the Public Health Services for Older Adults Under the Background of Government Purchasing: An Evolutionary Game Analysis Framework. *Frontiers in Public Health*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a principal-agent setting with information asymmetry, static punishment or reward mechanisms are insufficient to ensure full compliance (self-discipline) by service providers. Dynamic mechanisms, where the intensity of punishment or reward depends on the observed behavior, can stabilize higher levels of self-discipline. Specifically, higher punishment intensity increases the proportion of self-discipline, while higher reward intensity can decrease it due to moral hazard and adverse selection. The model is theoretical and does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results suggest that dynamic sanctioning mechanisms are more effective at promoting compliance than static ones.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (or self-discipline) depends on the design of the sanctioning mechanism, particularly whether it is static or dynamic. While the model is not a public goods game per se, it is structurally similar in that it models a social dilemma with potential for free-riding and the use of sanctions. The findings suggest that, in analogous PGG-like environments, dynamic punishment mechanisms are likely to be more effective at increasing cooperation than static ones, and that increasing punishment intensity generally increases compliance. However, the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. It is most useful for understanding the qualitative effects of punishment design and intensity on compliance behavior.

- source: Larson, J. M. (2017). WHY THE WEST BECAME WILD Informal Governance with Incomplete Networks. *World Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of peer punishment to sustain cooperation depends critically on the social network structure and the reach of gossip. Incomplete networks with peripheral members cannot sustain full cooperation: the most peripheral members are likely to be persistently cheated and ostracized, as their negative gossip does not reach enough punishers to deter defection. Shocks that increase the gains from defection or that create more peripheral positions (e.g., through rapid population growth or enclave settlement) lead to a ratcheting down of cooperation, with more members becoming persistent cheaters. The model generates hypotheses about when and where cooperation will break down, but does not provide quantitative predictions about group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how network structure and information transmission affect the limits of peer punishment in sustaining cooperation. It is relevant for understanding moderators of punishment effectiveness, especially the role of network incompleteness and peripheral positions. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that punishment will be less effective at increasing efficiency in settings with incomplete networks, high population turnover, or enclave settlement, but does not provide parameterized estimates or control-treatment efficiency comparisons.

- source: Ye, W. X., & Fan, S. H. (2020). Evolutionary traveler's dilemma game based on particle swarm optimization. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that using a PSO-based learning mechanism in the traveler's dilemma game leads to higher levels of cooperation (higher average claims) compared to traditional Fermi dynamics, across different network types. The effect of the reward/punishment parameter (R) and the balance between self-cognition and social cognition (c1) are explored: social learning promotes cooperation when the reward/punishment is small (large strategy interval), while self-cognition becomes more important as the reward/punishment increases (small strategy interval). The main outcome is the normalized cooperation level, not efficiency or total group payoff. The results are robust across network types, but the study is simulation-based and does not report direct efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how learning mechanisms and the structure of reward/punishment in a traveler's dilemma game (an adjacent but not exact variant of the public goods game) affect the prevalence of cooperative behavior (high claims). It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment/reward mechanisms in promoting cooperation depends on the size of the incentive and the learning dynamics. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes and studies a different game structure.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Wang, Y., & He, M. F. (2022). Impacts of special cooperation strategy with reward and punishment mechanism on cooperation evolution. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing special cooperators who can both reward and punish leads to a variety of stable states, including coexistence of all three strategies or pairs of strategies. Punishment is necessary to suppress defectors and enable cooperation, but excessive punishment can reduce the prevalence of special cooperators. Rewards only promote ordinary cooperation when punishment is at least moderate. The effects of reward and punishment on cooperation are non-linear: moderate levels are most effective, while too much of either can be counterproductive. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so findings are limited to behavioral strategy frequencies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence that punishment (especially when combined with reward) can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies in spatial evolutionary games. However, because the outcomes are proportions of strategy types rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The findings suggest that moderate punishment is most effective for promoting cooperation, and that the effect of reward depends on the level of punishment. For prediction of efficiency, this paper suggests that the relationship between punishment and efficiency may be non-monotonic, with moderate punishment being optimal, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Lee, H. W., Cleveland, C., & Szolnoki, A. (2024). Suppressing defection by increasing temptation: The impact of smart cooperators on a social dilemma situation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that adding a 'smart cooperator' strategy (who both punishes defectors and rewards cooperators, at a cost) to a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and smart cooperators. This cyclic dominance produces counter-intuitive effects: for example, increasing the temptation to defect can reduce the prevalence of defectors by indirectly supporting smart cooperators. Similarly, increasing punishment does not always reduce defection, and lowering the cost of smart cooperation can benefit unconditional cooperators more than smart cooperators. The main reported outcomes are the stationary fractions of each strategy, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how punishment and reward mechanisms interact in a spatial social dilemma with multiple strategies, but does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that the introduction of complex sanctioning strategies can lead to non-linear and sometimes counter-intuitive effects on cooperation and defection rates, but the lack of efficiency or payoff reporting limits its direct utility for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. The main value is in highlighting the potential for cyclic dominance and unexpected behavioral dynamics when multiple sanctioning strategies are present.

- source: Zhao, S. S., Pan, Q. H., Zhu, W. Q., & He, M. F. (2023). How punishing evil and promoting good promotes cooperation in social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of a 'punishing evil and promoting good' strategy can promote the survival of cooperation in social dilemmas. Increasing punishment strength alone can induce the emergence of this strategy and ordinary cooperators, but is not always sufficient to eliminate defectors. Increasing reward strength can shift the system from full defection to full cooperation when the temptation to defect is low, but is insufficient when temptation is high. The most robust promotion of cooperation occurs when both punishment and reward are present, with certain combinations of minimum values able to eliminate defectors entirely. The results are presented as equilibrium proportions of each strategy, not as efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that both punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in social dilemma settings, and that their effects are complementary. However, because the outcomes are reported as equilibrium strategy frequencies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it is a prisoner's dilemma variant), and the findings suggest that the presence and strength of punishment and reward mechanisms are important moderators of cooperation, but do not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency.

- source: Wang, R. Y., & Ng, C. N. (2015). Can Centralized Sanctioning Promote Trust in Social Dilemmas? A Two-Level Trust Game with Incomplete Information. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a centralized sanctioning authority into a repeated trust game with incomplete information can increase trust and reciprocity, especially when actors believe the authority is likely to punish defectors. The model shows that the threat of centralized punishment relaxes the conditions under which trust is placed and honored, compared to the baseline trust game without an authority. The effect is strongest when there is optimism about the authority's willingness to punish. The model predicts that centralized sanctioning can mitigate the end-game effect (decline in cooperation in final rounds). However, the analysis is theoretical and does not provide explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that adding a centralized punishment mechanism to a trust-based social dilemma can increase trust and cooperation, especially under incomplete information. While the model is not a public goods game, it is adjacent and relevant for predicting the effect of centralized punishment in repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling centralized punishment is likely to increase cooperation and, by implication, group payoffs or efficiency, particularly when the punishment is sufficiently strong and actors believe the authority is likely to punish. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency or group payoff, so its value for direct efficiency prediction is limited to qualitative inference.

- source: Su, F., Chang, J. B., & Li, X. (2021). Research on the Evolution Path and Influence Factors of Core Enterprise Oriented Entrepreneurship Ecosystem Under the Government Regulation. *Ieee Access*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and magnitude of punishment (by core enterprises to start-ups, and by government to both) increases the likelihood and speed of positive strategy selection (cooperation, entrepreneurship, regulation) among all agents. Conversely, higher costs (supervision, regulation, entrepreneurship) and higher subsidies can reduce the tendency for positive strategies. The model identifies multiple possible stable equilibria depending on parameter values, and shows that a moderately high, flexible reward and punishment mechanism is most effective for promoting positive strategies. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, focusing instead on the behavioral evolution of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms (and their cost/benefit structure) can promote positive, cooperative, or pro-social strategies in multi-agent systems similar to public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical results. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that increasing punishment (and reducing its cost) should increase the prevalence of positive strategies, which may be associated with higher efficiency in analogous PGG settings, but this is not directly demonstrated. The model's complexity and focus on behavioral strategy selection, rather than payoff, limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Shen, C., He, Z. X., Shi, L., Wang, Z., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Prosocial punishment bots breed social punishment in human players. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a fraction of bots that always cooperate and punish defectors (CP) into a population of normal players in a two-stage Prisoner's Dilemma can significantly increase the prevalence of prosocial punishment among normal players. This effect is stronger when bots are placed on high-degree nodes in scale-free networks or when players have a conformist bias (copying the majority). The effect is robust to different network structures, learning rules, and parameter values. However, the main reported outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies), not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not report the effect of punishment on efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the conditions under which punishment (specifically, prosocial punishment implemented by bots) can increase the prevalence of prosocial punishment and cooperation in a two-stage Prisoner's Dilemma. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding how network structure, imitation rules, and the presence of committed punishers affect the spread of prosocial punishment, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

  findings: The study finds that introducing peer pressure (punishment) into a spatial donation/extortion game can promote the prevalence of cooperation, especially at moderate punishment intensities. When punishment is too strong, extortioners dominate. The effect of punishment is non-monotonic: moderate punishment helps cooperation, but high punishment harms it. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy, not group payoff or efficiency. The findings are based on simulation, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence that punishment can promote cooperation in spatial evolutionary games with extortion strategies, but only within a certain range of punishment intensity. However, because the outcomes are frequencies of strategies rather than group payoff or efficiency, and because the model is a spatial donation/extortion game (not a standard public goods game), the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games, but it does suggest that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, possibly efficiency) is non-monotonic and context-dependent.

- source: Gao, D. D., & Guo, W. (2025). Evolutionary game and simulation analysis of tripartite subjects in public health emergencies under government reward and punishment mechanisms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that government reward and punishment mechanisms (subsidies and fines) for pharmaceutical companies and the public promote compliant production and cooperation in prevention and control, respectively. Increasing the magnitude of rewards or punishments accelerates the convergence to full compliance and cooperation. The initial willingness of parties to participate also affects the speed of convergence. However, the model does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and all results are in terms of the probability of adopting certain strategies and the speed of convergence to stable equilibria.

However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical results. The findings may inform the qualitative direction of punishment effects in similar environments, but cannot be used for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes as required by the downstream task.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Li, L., Shang, J., & Zhao, H. (2024). Periodic update rule with Q-learning promotes evolution of cooperation in transition with mechanism. *Neurocomputing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing a punishment mechanism, where cooperators can choose to become punishers at a cost, increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is strongest when the punishment cost is low and the update period for strategy revision is short. The Q-learning-based periodic update rule (switching between Fermi and aspiration-driven rules) further enhances cooperation. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is not a public goods game but a spatial pairwise prisoner's dilemma with punishment and learning dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas, especially when punishment is not too costly and agents update strategies frequently. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it should be used only as weak, adjacent evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The findings suggest that low punishment cost and frequent update opportunities are likely to make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation, but the translation to efficiency is uncertain.

However, the relationship is non-linear: too many powerful agents or excessively strong punishment can reduce cooperation. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical or experimental data.

However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment. The results suggest that the structure and exclusivity of punishment rights, as well as the intensity of punishment, can have non-monotonic effects on cooperation, which may be relevant for interpreting similar mechanisms in public goods games. Use this paper for qualitative or mechanistic insight, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing asymmetric carrot-stick strategies (where one population can reward or punish the other) leads to a rich set of evolutionary outcomes, including stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, bistability, and dominance, depending on parameter values. The presence and strength of punishment and reward (relative to benefit) are key moderators. The model suggests that asymmetric sanctions can help maintain cooperation and avoid the tragedy of the commons, especially when the cost of cooperation is high. However, the analysis is entirely theoretical and focuses on strategy frequencies and stability, not on group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the availability and strength of punishment and reward mechanisms (especially when asymmetric) can qualitatively change the evolutionary dynamics of cooperation. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that punishment (and reward) can stabilize cooperation under certain parameter regimes. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the presence and magnitude of punishment and reward (relative to benefit and cost) are important moderators, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment/reward can sustain cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Cardoso, F. M., Gracia-Lázaro, C., & Moreno, Y. (2020). Dynamics of heuristics selection for cooperative behaviour. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model shows that cooperation can emerge and be sustained in iterated Prisoner's Dilemma games when agents have memory and low mutation rates, with punishment and reciprocity as key mechanisms. Increasing memory increases cooperation, while higher mutation rates reduce it. When agents can discriminate kin, cooperation is still sustained, but the underlying heuristics shift from reciprocity to kin-based discrimination. The main reported outcomes are the fraction of cooperative actions and the distribution of strategies; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the importance of punishment and reciprocity in sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, especially when agents have memory and mutation rates are low. However, because the model is based on the Prisoner's Dilemma (not a public goods game or direct variant), and because efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that enabling punishment and memory can increase cooperation rates, but do not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments.

- source: Shutters, S. T. (2009). Strong reciprocity, social structure, and the evolution of fair allocations in a simulated ultimatum game. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a simulated ultimatum game, third-party punishment (altruistic punishment) does not generally lead to fair allocations, except in linearly structured populations where fair offers can evolve even without punishment. As the punishment multiplier (punishment impact per unit cost) increases, populations with spatial structure exhibit a sharp transition from low to very high offers, but these are not necessarily fair. The number of neighbors (social structure) strongly moderates both baseline offers and the threshold at which punishment induces high offers. The main outcome is the mean offer rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment cost-effectiveness and social structure (number of neighbors) affect behavioral outcomes (mean offer rates) in an ultimatum game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes from punishment in PGG-like environments. However, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in changing behavior is highly sensitive to the punishment cost/impact ratio and the social network structure. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the importance of considering punishment cost-effectiveness and group structure, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: Guo, T., Du, C. P., & Shi, L. (2024). Evolution of cooperation on interdependent networks: The impact of asymmetric punishment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The study finds that asymmetric punishment, where only one group can punish another and only across network layers, can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in spatial prisoner's dilemma games on interdependent networks. The effect is stronger when the punishment is more severe (higher fine per unit cost). When the temptation to defect is low, punishment is more effective than reward at promoting cooperation; when the temptation is high, reward becomes more effective. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The paper does not report efficiency or total group payoff, so its findings are most directly relevant to predicting cooperation rates rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in agent-based models of spatial prisoner's dilemma games with asymmetric punishment, increasing punishment strength or effectiveness increases cooperation rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that, in similar environments, enabling or strengthening punishment will likely increase cooperation rates, which may be correlated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly tested. The paper also shows that the effect of punishment depends on the temptation to defect and the cost-to-fine ratio. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding how punishment design (asymmetry, cost-to-fine ratio) affects cooperation rates, with only indirect implications for efficiency.

  findings: The paper finds that mutual punishment in the snowdrift game can either promote or inhibit cooperation depending on the temptation to defect and the punishment fine. For low temptation to defect, increasing punishment fine increases cooperation; for high temptation, it decreases cooperation; for intermediate temptation, there is a non-monotonic relationship. The main reported outcome is the steady-state fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The results are shown for both well-mixed and structured populations, with phase transitions in cooperation rates observed. The findings are relevant for understanding how mutual punishment (peer pressure) affects conformity and cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments with punishment. Its results are based on the snowdrift game, which is adjacent but not equivalent to the PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The mutual punishment mechanism is novel and may inform mechanism design, but the lack of payoff or efficiency analysis limits its direct applicability to the prediction task. The findings may be useful for understanding how punishment parameters affect cooperation rates, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

  findings: The study finds that punishment can promote cooperation in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma with reinforcement learning agents, but only if the punishment is sufficiently strong and not too costly. If the cost of punishment is high or the punishment intensity is low, introducing punishment can actually reduce cooperation below the baseline with no punishment. The system can exhibit both continuous and discontinuous transitions in cooperation as punishment parameters are varied. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; group efficiency or total payoff is not reported. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical data.

While the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, the results suggest that punishment does not always improve group outcomes: if punishment is too costly or too weak, it can reduce cooperation. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be non-monotonic and sensitive to cost-effectiveness. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings should be used as qualitative, mechanistic guidance rather than quantitative prediction.

  findings: The study finds that multi-step punishment (punishment lasting multiple time steps for low-reputation individuals) can promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game, but only if the punishment duration is neither too short nor too long. There is an optimal punishment time window that maximizes the frequency of cooperation. Too short punishment fails to deter defection, while too long punishment can backfire and reduce cooperation. The intensity of punishment (how much payoffs are reduced for punished individuals) is the dominant factor in promoting cooperation. The model does not include a cost for punishment, and does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. All results are based on the proportion of cooperators in the population.

The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation rates, especially with optimal duration and sufficient intensity, but the absence of punishment cost and lack of payoff analysis limit its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs and may inform qualitative expectations about punishment's effect on cooperation, but not on efficiency or welfare. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Xu, C., Hui, P. M., Yu, Y. Y., & Gu, G. Q. (2009). Self-organized cooperative behavior and critical penalty in an evolving population. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that in a population of agents playing a two-action game (cooperate or defect), increasing the penalty for defection increases the mean probability to cooperate. There is a critical penalty threshold, dependent on initial conditions, above which the population becomes fully cooperative. For populations with higher initial cooperation, less penalty is needed to achieve full cooperation, and in some cases, no penalty is required. The model also shows that after a population is trained to cooperate with penalty, the penalty can be relaxed without immediate loss of cooperation. The structure of the population (well-mixed vs. 2D lattice) affects the level of cooperation at intermediate penalties, with lattices supporting higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so the findings are not directly about payoff-based efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that increasing the severity of punishment can increase cooperation rates in repeated social dilemma games, with a critical threshold for full cooperation. However, because the main outcome is the mean probability to cooperate and not group payoff or efficiency, the results cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs and may inform qualitative expectations about the effect of punishment on cooperation, but not on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment can be effective in increasing cooperation, especially when initial cooperation is low, and that population structure can moderate this effect. For the prediction task, this paper is best used as indirect, qualitative support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation, but not as quantitative evidence for efficiency effects.

  findings: The study finds that a combined reward-punishment mechanism (RPM) in a dynamic network prisoner's dilemma can significantly increase the average frequency of cooperation compared to either pure punishment or pure reward alone. Pure punishment only promotes cooperation under high temptation to defect, while pure reward can actually undermine cooperation. The combination of both, with appropriately tuned intensities, sustains high cooperation even under strong temptation to defect. The model also shows that the RPM leads to spontaneous emergence of network heterogeneity, with cooperators becoming influential hubs. However, the main outcome is cooperation frequency, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence and calibration of both punishment and reward mechanisms can strongly affect cooperation rates in dynamic networked social dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a dynamic network prisoner's dilemma rather than a standard public goods game, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that in environments where both punishment and reward are available and appropriately balanced, cooperation (and likely efficiency) can be sustained at higher levels than with either mechanism alone, especially under high temptation to defect. The paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Wang, G. C., Su, Q., & Wang, L. (2022). Evolution of cooperation with joint liability. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that joint liability, when combined with costly punishment, strongly promotes the evolution of cooperation in a lending game. Punishment alone is insufficient to sustain cooperation, but joint liability provides incentives for individuals to punish defectors, leading to high levels of cooperation and low levels of defection. The condition for cooperation to evolve is relaxed from β−γ>4c (without joint liability) to β>γ (with joint liability and low tolerability for defection). The effect is robust to model extensions, including intragroup donation games (Prisoner's Dilemma). The abundance of cooperators and punishers is maximized at intermediate values of selection intensity, population size, and mutation rate. The model is adjacent to public goods games but differs in structure and outcome measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the combination of joint liability and costly punishment can greatly increase cooperation in group settings similar to, but not identical with, public goods games. The findings suggest that punishment is much more effective at promoting cooperation when joint liability is present, and that the threshold for punishment to be effective is lower. However, because the model is not a standard PGG and does not report efficiency as a ratio of realized to maximum group payoff, the results should be transferred to PGG efficiency predictions with caution. The paper is most informative for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment and group-level liability interact, and for predicting that punishment will be more effective in environments with joint liability or similar group-level consequences.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., Nie, S. L., & Wang, L. (2015). Emergence of parochial altruism in well-mixed populations of multiple groups. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that parochial altruism is more likely to emerge and be favored by selection when intergroup punishment is more effective (higher fine-to-cost ratio), when there are more groups (higher assortment), and when mutation rates are low. Affiliation (belonging to more groups) makes parochial altruism less likely for low mutation, but more likely for high mutation. The model provides explicit conditions (in terms of benefit, cost, punishment fine, and punishment cost) for when parochial altruism is favored. However, the main outcomes are about the evolutionary abundance of strategies, not about group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment can promote the emergence of cooperation-like strategies in group-structured populations, especially when punishment is effective and group assortment is high. However, because the model is theoretical and focuses on evolutionary stability of strategies rather than direct efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results suggest that punishment is more likely to be beneficial (in terms of promoting cooperation-like behavior) when punishment is effective and group structure is strong, but the translation to efficiency outcomes in experimental PGGs is indirect.

- source: Wang, Y. F., & Meng, X. Z. (2023). Evolutionary game dynamics of cooperation in prisoner?s dilemma with time delay. *Mathematical Biosciences and Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a 2-player prisoner's dilemma, adding a penalty for mutual defection (punishment) leads to stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors when the penalty exceeds the cost of cooperation (a > c). The equilibrium proportion of cooperators increases with the penalty size. When time delays are introduced, the system can undergo a Hopf bifurcation: for small delays, the equilibrium is stable, but for delays above a critical threshold (which decreases as penalty increases), the system oscillates between cooperation and defection. Adding mutation (random switching between strategies) slightly reduces the equilibrium proportion of cooperators, but does not fundamentally change the qualitative dynamics. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the frequency of cooperation and the stability of these frequencies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that increasing the severity of punishment for defection in a prisoner's dilemma increases the equilibrium proportion of cooperators, which is typically associated with higher group payoff in such games. However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so the findings must be interpreted as indirect evidence. The results suggest that, in similar 2-player games, enabling punishment (with sufficient severity) will increase cooperation rates, and thus likely improve efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline. However, if time delays are present and large, the system may become unstable and oscillate, which could reduce average efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings with 2 players, no communication, continuous contribution/strategy choice, and deterministic evolutionary dynamics. The results are less directly transferable to standard public goods games with more players, explicit payoff reporting, or experimental data.

- source: Woodcock, S., & Heath, J. (2002). The robustness of altruism as an evolutionary strategy. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment mechanisms, such as enforcement and ostracism, dramatically increase the robustness of altruism in evolutionary simulations of the prisoner's dilemma. While group selection and imitation can help sustain altruism under certain conditions (e.g., small group size, high imitation rates), punishment allows altruism to persist in much larger groups and under a wider range of conditions. The key mechanism is that punishment increases correlation among altruists, allowing them to interact more with each other and less with defectors. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare as outcomes; it focuses on the evolutionary persistence of altruism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that punishment mechanisms can greatly increase the persistence of altruism (cooperation) in evolutionary environments similar to the prisoner's dilemma. However, because the main outcome is the proportion of altruists rather than group efficiency or payoff, the findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency in public goods games. The results suggest that, all else equal, adding punishment mechanisms is likely to increase cooperation and thus could plausibly increase efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this effect in payoff terms. The design dimensions most relevant here are the presence and effectiveness of punishment, group size, and the structure of interaction (e.g., group partitioning, imitation).

- source: Bausch, A. W. (2014). Evolving intergroup cooperation. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in-group policing is highly effective at promoting inter-group cooperation in an evolutionary Prisoner's Dilemma model, with high levels of CC outcomes (over 80% in many runs). In contrast, collective sanctions alone do not increase cooperation above baseline and can even reduce it when combined with in-group policing, especially when the sanction period is long. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that agent-level punishment is more effective than group-level punishment for sustaining cooperation. The findings are robust across a range of parameterizations, but are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than direct payoff or efficiency measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with group structure, agent-level punishment (in-group policing) is much more effective at sustaining cooperation than group-level punishment (collective sanctions). However, because the model does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game, its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited. The results are most useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment can promote or hinder cooperation, and for anticipating that group-level punishment may not translate into higher efficiency or payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence of in-group policing (individualized punishment) is likely to have a more positive effect on cooperation than collective sanctions, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: Jaffe, K. (2008). Evolution of Shame as an Adaptation to Social Punishment and its Contribution to Social Cohesiveness. *Complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that shame can evolve as an adaptation to social punishment, increasing pro-social behavior (generosity) after punishment. When punishment is present (especially if costless to the punisher) or when shame is present, populations maintain higher levels of generosity and a balance between punishers and non-punishers. However, the model does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the evolutionary dynamics of behavioral traits. The results suggest that shame and punishment can stabilize cooperation for long periods, but with fluctuations between cooperative and selfish phases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (and shame) can increase pro-social behavior in resource-sharing environments, which may translate to higher efficiency in public goods games. However, because the model is not a direct PGG and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The main relevance is in showing that punishment and shame can stabilize cooperation, especially when punishment is not costly to the punisher. The findings are more about evolutionary stability and trait frequencies than about efficiency per se.

- source: Cofnas, N. (2018). POWER IN CULTURAL EVOLUTION AND THE SPREAD OF PROSOCIAL NORMS. *Quarterly Review of Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that mainstream cultural evolutionary models, which treat punishment as an individual-level, uncoordinated action, fail to capture the collective, power-based enforcement of prosocial norms observed in real-world societies, especially among hunter-gatherers. It contends that collective punishment and coordinated norm enforcement are central to the evolution of morality and prosociality, and that these processes are not well-modeled by standard public goods game frameworks. The paper reviews anthropological evidence for collective punishment and discusses how this shapes moral psychology, but does not report or synthesize efficiency or payoff outcomes from experimental games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical context for interpreting the role of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments, emphasizing that real-world punishment is often collective and coordinated, not merely the sum of individual actions. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze how game design dimensions (such as player count, rounds, or punishment cost) moderate these effects. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that models which ignore collective decision-making and power dynamics may misrepresent the real impact of punishment, but it does not provide data or direct evidence to calibrate predictions of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Kritikos, A., & Bolle, F. (2004). Punishment as a public good. When should monopolists care about a consumer boycott?. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of consumers to punish a monopolist (e.g., via boycott) and thereby reduce prices depends on the number of consumers and the degree of solidarity among them. With few consumers, individual punishment can be effective and lead to lower prices. As the number of consumers increases, the incentive to individually punish diminishes (public good problem), and the monopolist can more easily charge higher (Cournot) prices unless solidarity is high. The model predicts that successful collective punishment (boycott) is more likely in small groups or when solidarity is strong, and less likely in large, uncoordinated groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how group size and solidarity affect the effectiveness of punishment in public-good-like environments, specifically in the context of consumer boycotts against monopolists. While it does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, it suggests that increasing group size without mechanisms for solidarity or coordination will reduce the effectiveness of punishment in improving group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment is less likely to improve efficiency in large, uncoordinated groups, but may be effective in small groups or when solidarity is high. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its quantitative predictive value.

- source: Midlarsky, M. I. (2004). Nihilism in Political Chaos: Himmler, bin Laden, and Altruistic Punishment. *Studies in Conflict & Terrorism*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that in the absence of effective political institutions, groups may achieve cooperation and unity through costly punishment of defectors, a process the author terms 'altruistic punishment.' Drawing on public goods game experiments (Fehr & Gächter, 2002), the author claims that the availability of punishment increases cooperation, and extends this logic to explain extreme forms of group cohesion and violence in historical and contemporary political movements. However, the paper does not report any new empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation in group settings, referencing public goods game experiments as evidence. However, it does not provide empirical data, efficiency outcomes, or direct analysis of game design dimensions. Its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited to its summary of prior experimental findings (e.g., Fehr & Gächter, 2002), not to any new or directly relevant evidence.

- source: Nakamaru, M. (2006). Lattice models in ecology and social sciences. *Ecological Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews and synthesizes theoretical and simulation results showing that lattice-structured populations (as opposed to well-mixed populations) can promote the evolution of cooperation and punishment (Altruist-Punisher), as well as spiteful behavior, depending on the model's updating rules. In particular, the Altruist-Punisher strategy can invade selfish populations more easily in lattice models, and punishment is more effective in promoting cooperation in these settings. However, the paper does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on the evolutionary success of strategies and the spread of norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that spatial or network structure (such as lattice models) can moderate the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper's findings are only adjacent: they suggest that punishment may be more effective in structured populations, but do not quantify the effect on efficiency or provide data on payoff outcomes. The paper is more useful for understanding mechanisms and contextual moderators than for direct prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Abramson, N. R., & Senyshyn, Y. (2010). Effective Punishment Through Forgiveness: Rediscovering Kierkegaard's Knight of Faith in the Abraham Story. *Organization Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that punishment, when combined with forgiveness and a commitment to reconciliation (as in the Knight of Faith model), is more likely to restore trust and effective relationships than traditional retributive approaches. It draws on philosophical analysis, biblical narrative (the Abraham story), and real-world examples (South African Truth and Reconciliation Commission vs. Sarbanes-Oxley Act) to argue that restorative justice processes can lead to repentance and reconciliation, whereas retributive punishment alone often leads to retaliation, distrust, and ongoing conflict. However, these findings are conceptual and interpretive, not empirical or quantitative.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative data relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games or similar environments. Its value for the prediction task is limited to conceptual insights: it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and restoring group functioning may depend on the presence of forgiveness and opportunities for reconciliation, rather than punishment alone. However, it does not address or manipulate the game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Molho, C., De Petrillo, F., Garfield, Z. H., & Slewe, S. (2024). Cross-societal variation in norm enforcement systems. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper reviews 28 empirical studies on cross-societal variation in punishment and norm enforcement, covering a range of methods (economic games, vignettes, ethnography, surveys). It finds that punishment and norm enforcement are present in many societies, but their prevalence, forms, and social acceptance vary widely. Socio-ecological factors (community size, pathogen prevalence, subsistence type) and cultural factors (individualism/collectivism, honor, kinship intensity, power distance, trust) are discussed as moderators of punishment behaviors. The review notes that evidence on these moderators is often mixed or context-dependent. While some included studies use public goods games with punishment, the review does not aggregate or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and attitudinal measures of punishment and norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical and contextual background on how punishment and norm enforcement vary across societies and what factors may moderate these behaviors. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. For the downstream prediction task, this review may help identify potentially relevant moderators (e.g., community size, cultural values) but does not supply parameter-level evidence or efficiency outcomes needed for direct prediction. It is best used for contextual framing and hypothesis generation rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

It highlights that PD patients, especially those with impulse-control disorders and on dopaminergic medication, may show altered punishment behavior. However, it does not provide data on efficiency or group payoff, and its main focus is on the mechanisms and behavioral expressions of punishment rather than on payoff-based outcomes.

As such, it offers limited direct support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment from game design dimensions, but may be useful for understanding potential moderators (e.g., impulsivity, neurobiological state) in special populations.

- source: Chassang, S., & Takahashi, S. (2011). Robustness to incomplete information in repeated games. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoners' dilemma games, the set of robustly sustainable equilibrium payoffs (i.e., efficiency) is sharply affected by concerns about incomplete information. Under complete information, grim-trigger strategies can sustain cooperation if the discounted value of future cooperation exceeds the temptation to defect. However, when robustness to incomplete information is required, the cost of being the 'sucker' (cooperating while the other defects) becomes critical, and the set of sustainable payoffs can shrink dramatically. Selective-punishment strategies, which punish only the deviator, are more robust than grim-trigger strategies, and can sustain cooperation under a wider range of parameters. The paper provides explicit characterizations of when efficient cooperation is robustly sustainable and when only inefficient outcomes (permanent defection) are robust. The main outcomes are theoretical characterizations of efficiency/welfare as a function of game parameters and strategy types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated prisoners' dilemma–like games under incomplete information. It shows that the design of the punishment mechanism (grim-trigger vs. selective punishment), the cost of being punished, and the enforceability of asymmetric outcomes are critical moderators. If only symmetric cooperation (CC) is enforceable, robust efficiency collapses to permanent defection. If asymmetric outcomes (DC or CD) are enforceable, robust efficiency can be as high as in the complete information case. The results are directly about efficiency/welfare, not just contribution rates. For prediction, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the details of the punishment scheme and the game's payoff structure, especially under incomplete information. However, the results are for repeated prisoners' dilemma, not public goods games per se, so transfer to PGGs should be done with caution.

- source: Röhl, T., Röhl, C., Schuster, H. G., & Traulsen, A. (2007). Impact of fraud on the mean-field dynamics of cooperative social systems. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a reputation-based cooperation system (image scoring), the introduction of fraud (scammers who fake cooperation) and punishment (penalties for detected fraud) leads to complex dynamics. If the probability of detecting fraud and the penalty for fraud are high enough, cooperation (discriminators) can dominate. For intermediate detection probabilities, a limited presence of scammers can actually increase the basin of attraction for cooperation, making it easier for cooperation to evolve. The number of rounds of interaction is a key moderator: with more rounds, the payoff advantage of cooperation increases. The model provides explicit conditions (in terms of cost, benefit, penalty, detection probability, and rounds) for when each strategy is favored. The overall effect of punishment on efficiency is mixed: it can increase efficiency by suppressing fraud, but the presence of some fraud can also facilitate cooperation under certain conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in reputation-based cooperation games depends on the detection probability, penalty size, cost-benefit ratio, and number of rounds. In games with more rounds and higher detection/penalty, punishment is more likely to increase efficiency by supporting cooperation. However, a limited amount of undetected fraud can sometimes increase the basin of attraction for cooperation, so the relationship is not strictly monotonic. The model is not a standard public goods game, but its structure and payoff logic are closely related. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the efficiency impact of punishment will be more positive when detection is high, penalties are large, and the game is repeated over more rounds. The presence of fraud and its detectability are important moderators.

- source: Qian, X. H., Huang, M., Zhang, Q. Y., Yin, M. Q., & Wang, X. W. (2018). Mechanism design of incentive-based reverse auctions with loss-averse 3PLs under incomplete attributes. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that combining incentive-based contracts (cost-sharing) with contingent penalties (punishment for delivery failure) in reverse auctions increases the ex ante expected profit (efficiency) of the system and the principal, compared to standard reverse auctions without these mechanisms. The efficiency gains are moderated by the number of agents (more agents increase efficiency), the fraction of low-risk agents, and the degree of loss aversion (higher loss aversion reduces efficiency). The presence and magnitude of the penalty (punishment) is a key design variable, and its optimal value depends on delivery risk parameters. The model provides explicit formulas for optimal efficiency under different mechanism designs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that introducing punishment (contingent penalty) in a competitive procurement game with cost uncertainty and risk can increase system efficiency, but the effect is moderated by agent risk aversion and the number of agents. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that, all else equal, adding a punishment mechanism to a reverse auction or similar environment will increase efficiency, especially when there are more agents and when agents are less loss-averse. However, the magnitude of the effect depends on the specific design of the punishment and incentive parameters. The findings are most directly applicable to procurement/auction settings but are informative for adjacent public-goods-like environments.

- source: Choi, J. K. (2007). Trembles may support cooperation in a repeated prisoner's dilemma game. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
