  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of trembles (errors) in repeated prisoner's dilemma games can support the evolutionary stability of conditional cooperation (punishment-based strategies), but only when the benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation is not too high. Specifically, if the benefit of cooperation is less than or equal to twice the cost (b ≤ 2c), then the ALLT (all trigger) equilibrium is asymptotically stable for sufficiently high continuation probability and low error rate. If the benefit is too high (b > 2c), then the equilibrium becomes unstable, and unconditional cooperators can invade, undermining the effectiveness of punishment. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using explicit payoff calculations and stability conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in supporting cooperation (and thus efficiency) in repeated prisoner's dilemma-like environments depends critically on the benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation and the presence of errors. For prediction tasks, it suggests that punishment will only improve efficiency when the benefit-to-cost ratio is not too high and when errors are present but not too frequent. The results are most directly applicable to two-player repeated games with trigger strategies, but the logic may extend to public goods games with similar strategic structure. The findings highlight that increasing the benefit of cooperation does not always make punishment more effective; in fact, it can undermine stability if too large. The paper does not provide direct evidence for multi-player public goods games, but the mechanisms discussed are relevant for adjacent settings.

- source: Nakamura, M., & Ohtsuki, H. (2014). Indirect reciprocity in three types of social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player simultaneous-move social dilemmas, the stability and efficiency of cooperation via indirect reciprocity depend on the structure of the social norm (reputation updating rule) and the underlying game. In Snowdrift and Prisoner's Dilemma, strong punishment of defectors (assigning bad reputations for defection against good players) is necessary to maintain high efficiency (i.e., stable cooperation with high group payoff). In Stag Hunt, such punishment is less critical, and norms that unfairly favor reciprocators (always assign good reputation to reciprocation) can enhance the robustness and efficiency of cooperation. The paper also shows that punishing unconditional cooperators (who help bad players) is important for maintaining efficient cooperation in all three games. The efficiency outcome (i.e., group payoff relative to full cooperation) is maximized under certain social norms, but the effect of punishment-like mechanisms is context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment (via reputation loss) on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the underlying game structure and the details of the punishment mechanism (social norm). In games with strong temptation to defect (SG, PD), enabling punishment (bad reputation for defectors) increases efficiency by stabilizing cooperation. In coordination games (SH), such punishment is less necessary, and norms that favor reciprocators can be more effective. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency should be moderated by the type of social dilemma and the specific implementation of the punishment/reputation mechanism. The results are most directly relevant to two-player games, not standard multi-player PGGs, but the logic may transfer to small-group settings with reputation-based punishment.

- source: Nuño, J. C., Herrero, M. A., & Primicerio, M. (2010). Fighting cheaters: How and how much to invest. *European Journal of Applied Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that in the absence of anti-cheating expenditure, cheaters dominate and total wealth collapses. Introducing and increasing expenditure to fight cheating (via social or punitive means) reduces the cheater population and increases total wealth, but only up to an optimal point. Beyond this, further expenditure reduces wealth due to the cost of enforcement. The optimal strategy involves a balance between social promotion and deterrence, with the best compromise (for the parameter values used) being to spend about 5% of wealth on anti-cheating measures, allocating about 90% of that to social promotion. The model also shows that the effectiveness of punishment (police efficiency) is a critical moderator: if punishment is highly effective, more resources should be allocated to it; if not, social measures are preferable. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and are supported by sensitivity analyses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment or anti-cheating measures in a public-goods-like environment increases efficiency (total wealth) up to an optimal point, after which further increases in punishment expenditure can reduce efficiency. The model highlights the importance of both the amount spent and the effectiveness of punishment. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (or anti-cheating expenditure) will increase efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline, but the magnitude depends on the cost, effectiveness, and allocation of resources. The model also warns that excessive punishment spending can be counterproductive. While the model is not an experimental PGG, its structure and outcomes are closely analogous and informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Xu, J., Song, Y. B., & van der Schaar, M. (2014). Sharing in Networks of Strategic Agents. *Ieee Journal of Selected Topics in Signal Processing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that distributed rating-based sanctioning protocols can fully or nearly fully restore efficiency in repeated network sharing games with strategic agents, provided certain incentive compatibility conditions are met. When the network's connectivity is not too high and agents' discount factors are sufficient, the protocol achieves the social optimum (PoA=1). As network connectivity increases or agents become more impatient, efficiency declines but remains higher than with direct reciprocity mechanisms. The protocol is robust to imperfect monitoring and can be tuned for dynamic networks. Theoretical and simulation results show that the protocol outperforms Tit-for-Tat and similar direct punishment schemes, especially in heterogeneous or asymmetric networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that distributed, rating-based punishment mechanisms can substantially increase efficiency in repeated networked cooperation environments, often achieving the social optimum. The effect is most pronounced when the network is not too highly connected and agents are sufficiently patient. The protocol's performance is robust to imperfect monitoring and network dynamics. For prediction tasks, the presence of a distributed, indirect punishment mechanism (ratings affecting future access) is a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially compared to direct, bilateral punishment. The results are most directly applicable to networked repeated games with costly sharing, which are adjacent to but not identical to standard public goods games.

- source: O'Connor, C. (2016). The Evolution of Guilt: A Model-Based Approach. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that guilt, modeled as a behavioral trait leading to cooperation, apology, and reparative actions, can be individually adaptive in evolutionary games. In both stag hunt and prisoner's dilemma settings, guilt-proneness increases the likelihood of cooperation, which in turn raises individual and group payoffs. When punishment for defection is present, guilt-proneness further increases individual fitness by reducing the risk of being punished. The models show that costly or honest signals of apology (as a proxy for guilt) can stabilize cooperation and prevent the invasion of defectors or 'fakers.' The efficiency of the group (measured as average fitness or payoff) is higher in populations where guilt-proneness and apology are present, especially when punishment mechanisms exist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms, when combined with behavioral traits like guilt-proneness and apology, can increase efficiency (group payoff) in social dilemma games. While it does not directly model public goods games, the findings are adjacent and suggest that enabling punishment and supporting honest signals of intent (like apology) are likely to increase efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment. The models also highlight the importance of the cost and credibility of punishment and apology mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that introducing punishment (especially with some cost or honest signaling) will increase efficiency, provided that the game environment allows for reciprocity and repeated interaction.

- source: Handfield, T., Huang, P. H., & Simpson, R. M. (2016). Climate change, cooperation and moral bioenhancement. *Journal of Medical Ethics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that enhancing retributive (punishment) tendencies in social dilemma games (including public goods and prisoner's dilemma paradigms) is likely to lower group efficiency, even if it increases cooperation rates. The authors review theoretical models and cite experimental evidence indicating that harsh punishment can make groups poorer, and that those who punish often gain less than those who do not. The effect of punishment on efficiency is sensitive to the cost/benefit ratio but relatively insensitive to punishment severity. The paper also notes that anti-social punishment (punishing cooperators) can be highly destructive to cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and literature-based evidence that enabling or increasing punishment in public-goods-game-like environments is likely to reduce group efficiency, especially when punishment is harsh or misapplied. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, adding or intensifying punishment mechanisms may not increase and may even decrease efficiency, despite possible increases in cooperation rates. The effect is particularly negative when punishment is severe or when anti-social punishment is possible. The paper is less informative about the precise quantitative effect sizes but is clear about the direction of the effect on efficiency.

- source: Verma, P., Nandi, A. K., & Sengupta, S. (2017). Bribery games on inter-dependent regular networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the spread of honest officers (and thus reduction in bribery) is favored over a larger region of parameter space compared to mixed populations. However, the effect of network connectivity is non-monotonic: increasing the degree of the citizen network initially helps honest strategies, but beyond an optimal point, further increases hinder the spread of honesty. Similarly, the degree of the interaction network (citizen-officer links) has an optimal value for maximizing the prevalence of honest officers. Asymmetry in population sizes (more citizens per officer) makes it harder to eradicate bribery. The cost of complaining and the bribe amount are key parameters: lower cost of complaining and lower bribe amounts favor honest strategies. The main dependent variables are the fixation probability of honest officers and the phase space area where honest strategies dominate, not direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it analyzes how network structure, punishment asymmetry, and behavioral parameters affect the prevalence of honest (non-corrupt) strategies. In public-goods-like environments with asymmetric punishment, structured populations can reduce corruption, but only up to an optimal level of network connectivity. The findings suggest that, for similar games, enabling punishment (asymmetric, targeting only one side) in a structured population can increase the prevalence of socially beneficial behavior, but the effect depends on network connectivity and behavioral costs. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that punishment can improve outcomes (in terms of honest behavior) in structured settings, but warns that too much connectivity can undermine this effect. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured, the findings should be used as indirect evidence.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Wang, Y., Chen, Q., Gao, L. Y., & He, M. F. (2022). Effects of quasi-defection strategy on cooperation evolution in social dilemma. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of quasi-defectors—who can both reward cooperators and punish defectors—enables the stable coexistence of cooperation and quasi-defection, and can suppress defection under certain parameter regimes. The effect of punishment on cooperation is positive but limited: increasing punishment initially increases cooperation, but further increases have diminishing returns. Reward can compensate for the limited effect of punishment and further promote cooperation. The cost of rewarding/punishing must be low for quasi-defectors to persist and support cooperation. The model is theoretical and does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results suggest that mechanisms combining reward and punishment can sustain higher levels of cooperation than punishment alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that combining punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in social dilemmas, and that the effect of punishment on cooperation is positive but limited by cost and diminishing returns. However, because the model is based on a prisoner's dilemma with a quasi-defection strategy and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games is limited. The findings suggest that, in analogous settings, moderate punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency would depend on the cost structure and the mapping from strategy proportions to group payoff.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2022). Advanced defensive cooperators promote cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing advanced defensive cooperators, who can identify and punish anti-defensive defectors at a cost, leads to higher equilibrium fractions of cooperators compared to the standard prisoner's dilemma. The presence of advanced defensive cooperators is especially important when the temptation to defect is high. The model shows that the effectiveness of advanced defensive cooperation depends on parameters such as the temptation value, defensive benefit, defensive loss, fine (punishment), and screening cost. While the model tracks payoffs in the replicator equations, the main reported results are the fractions of each strategy at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, advanced defensive cooperation with screening and fines) can increase the prevalence of cooperation in social dilemma settings. However, because the main outcomes are strategy fractions rather than group efficiency or total payoff, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task of forecasting efficiency. The model is a prisoner's dilemma variant, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff. Thus, while the findings suggest that enabling punishment can increase cooperation, they do not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes in public-goods-game-like environments.

- source: Rosas, A. (2008). Multilevel selection and human altruism. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that the evolution of human altruism depends on the degree to which individuals (or their social/psychological mechanisms) can control assortative grouping, such as by punishing defectors and maintaining group cohesion. It claims that in human societies, mechanisms like punishment, reputation, and social norms can, in principle, prevent subversion from within and make altruism individually adaptive. The discussion references public goods games and the importance of punishment for sustaining cooperation, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and social mechanisms might enable stable cooperation in public goods settings by engineering assortative grouping and deterring free-riders. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or variants. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of mechanisms (like punishment and reputation) that allow groups to control subversion from within, suggesting that such mechanisms could, in principle, improve group outcomes. For direct prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in experimental public goods games, this paper is not directly informative.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2021). Cooperation evolves by the payoff-difference-based probabilistic reward. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a probabilistic reward mechanism, where the probability of rewarding a cooperator increases with the payoff difference between the player and the cooperator, leads to higher average payoffs and more cooperation in most cases. The effect is robust across regular, random, and scale-free network topologies, though the strength and range of the effect depend on the network structure and the reward parameter t. The paper does not directly study punishment in this model, but situates its findings in the context of prior work on punishment and reward in public goods and prisoner's dilemma games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that a well-designed reward mechanism can increase group efficiency (average payoff) in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, especially under certain network structures and parameter ranges. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, nor does it manipulate or compare punishment and reward within the same experimental or simulation framework. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by analogy and through its discussion of prior punishment studies.

- source: Pérez-Escudero, A., Friedman, J., & Gore, J. (2016). Preferential interactions promote blind cooperation and informed defection. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games where one player can gather information before cooperating or defecting, aversion to information gathering (i.e., a preference for 'blind' cooperation) can be sustained either by punishment (ending the game if the player looks) or by preferential interactions (continuing only with reliable partners). In homogeneous populations, punishment is required to enforce blind cooperation and thus higher payoffs. In heterogeneous populations, preferential interactions can achieve similar or greater efficiency without punishment. The model also predicts that if detecting information gathering is costly, punishment-based mechanisms break down in homogeneous populations, but preferential interactions remain effective in heterogeneous ones. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore positive in homogeneous populations but redundant or unnecessary in heterogeneous ones.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated cooperation games depends on population heterogeneity. In homogeneous populations, punishment (ending the game after information gathering or defection) is necessary to enforce cooperation and increase efficiency. In heterogeneous populations, preferential partner selection can achieve high efficiency without punishment. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, one should consider the degree of population heterogeneity and the possibility of preferential interactions. If the environment is homogeneous and punishment is strong, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency. If the environment is heterogeneous and allows for preferential interactions, punishment may have little or no additional effect on efficiency.

- source: Fishman, M. A. (2006). Involuntary defection and the evolutionary origins of empathy. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated dyadic cooperation games with involuntary defection, unconditional punishment (retaliation for any defection) undermines cooperation and reduces efficiency, as it leads to cycles of mutual defection. In contrast, an empathic retaliator strategy—where punishment is withheld if the opponent's defection follows one's own involuntary defection—can be evolutionarily stable and support high efficiency, especially when the expected number of repeated interactions is large. The stability and efficiency of cooperation depend on the cost of helping, the probability of being able to help, and the expected number of interaction cycles. The paper provides explicit conditions and payoff matrices for these outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms critically affects efficiency in repeated cooperation games. Specifically, unconditional punishment (retaliation) in the presence of involuntary defection leads to lower efficiency, while empathic (conditional) punishment can sustain high efficiency if repeated interactions are frequent. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on whether punishment is unconditional or conditional/empathic, the frequency of repeated interactions, and the possibility of involuntary defection. However, the model is not a public goods game, and punishment is not costly in the sense of standard PGG experiments; rather, it is refusal to help. Thus, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs should be made with caution, but the qualitative insight is that punishment can either harm or help efficiency depending on its design and the game context.

- source: Deb, J., & González-Díaz, J. (2019). Enforcing social norms: Trust-building and community enforcement. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The main finding is that, in large enough populations and with sufficiently patient players, efficient (cooperative) outcomes can be sustained in equilibrium in repeated anonymous random matching games, even when players only observe their own transactions and have no information about others' actions or identities. The mechanism involves a trust-building phase followed by community enforcement (contagion-based punishment). The set of achievable equilibrium payoffs includes those arbitrarily close to the efficient frontier, as long as the stage game has a strict Nash equilibrium and a one-sided incentive profile. The paper provides detailed conditions and mathematical proofs for when and how this is possible.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical support that, in repeated games with random matching, minimal information, and community enforcement (contagion-based punishment), the introduction of a punishment mechanism (in the form of Nash reversion triggered by observed deviations) can enable the group to achieve efficiency levels arbitrarily close to full cooperation, provided the population is large enough and players are sufficiently patient. The results are not specific to the public goods game but apply to a broad class of two-player games with certain properties. The findings suggest that, in environments with similar structure (random matching, minimal information, repeated play, and the possibility of community-wide punishment), enabling punishment can have a strongly positive effect on efficiency, even when direct monitoring is not possible. However, the results are theoretical and depend on equilibrium reasoning, not empirical observation.

- source: Raub, W., Buskens, V., & Frey, V. (2019). Strategic tie formation for long-term exchange relations. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that when actors can invest in strategic tie formation (i.e., commit to repeated interaction at a cost), and when the continuation probability and payoff structure are favorable, they will rationally choose to form ties and cooperate, resulting in higher group efficiency (payoff) than in the absence of such ties, where defection prevails. The efficiency gain from tie formation is explicitly quantified as the difference in equilibrium payoffs between mutual cooperation and mutual defection, net of tie formation costs. The model also generalizes to other social dilemma games (e.g., Trust Game) and discusses the role of institutional design in facilitating or hindering efficient outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling mechanisms for repeated interaction and voluntary commitment (analogous to endogenous punishment or sanctioning institutions) can substantially increase efficiency in social dilemma environments, compared to control conditions without such mechanisms. While the model is not a direct PGG or experimental study, its formal results on equilibrium payoffs, tie formation, and cooperation are highly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in repeated, two-player social dilemmas with commitment options. The explicit mapping of efficiency gains to game parameters (payoff structure, continuation probability, cost of commitment) can inform predictions about the impact of punishment-like or commitment-enabling design features in related experimental games.

- source: Gutnisky, D. A., & Zanutto, B. S. (2004). Cooperation in the iterated prisoner's dilemma is learned by operant conditioning mechanisms. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The operant conditioning model (ZL) can learn to cooperate in the IPD and achieves high average payoffs against a variety of strategies. In evolutionary simulations, ZL can dominate or survive in populations depending on the mix of strategies and the presence of noise (random moves). However, ZL is always slightly less efficient than non-learning strategies because it requires time to learn optimal responses. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on reinforcement learning from payoffs. The findings are relevant for understanding how learning mechanisms can support cooperation and efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, but do not directly address the effect of enabling or disabling punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide insight into how learning mechanisms (operant conditioning) can support cooperation and high payoffs in repeated dyadic dilemmas (IPD). The absence of explicit punishment or sanctioning means that the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that learning-based strategies can achieve high efficiency, but may be less efficient than fixed strategies in the short run due to learning costs. For prediction tasks focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency, this paper offers only indirect, contextual support.

- source: Murase, Y., & Hilbe, C. (2024). Computational evolution of social norms in well-mixed and group-structured populations. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in well-mixed populations, even with complex (third-order) social norms, cooperation and high group payoff (efficiency) are difficult to sustain: defecting strategies (ALLD) are most abundant, and average payoffs are low. In group-structured populations, however, cooperation and high efficiency can evolve and persist, especially when third-order norms are available. The most successful norm (L1) treats cooperation as always good and defection as bad, except when defection is 'justified' (against bad recipients). This norm is robust to disagreements and errors in private assessment. The results highlight the importance of population structure and norm complexity for the evolution of efficient, cooperative outcomes. The model does not implement explicit costly punishment, but some norms functionally resemble punishment by allowing justified defection without reputational cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the population (well-mixed vs. group-structured) and the complexity of available social norms (second-order vs. third-order) are critical moderators of the effect of reputational 'punishment' (justified defection) on group efficiency. In well-mixed populations, enabling more complex norms or reputational punishment does not reliably increase efficiency; in group-structured populations, it can lead to high efficiency. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of group structure and the richness of the norm/strategy space are key predictors of whether enabling reputational punishment will improve efficiency. However, the results may not transfer directly to standard PGGs with explicit costly punishment, as the model is dyadic and punishment is reputational, not material.

- source: Rob, R., & Yang, H. X. (2010). Long-term relationships as safeguards. *Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that endogenous relationship formation and dissolution can sustain cooperation and high efficiency in a repeated prisoner's dilemma, but only if the fraction of always-defect (bad) types is neither too low nor too high. When the fraction of bad types is in an intermediate range, a 'good equilibrium' exists in which strategic players cooperate, and social welfare is maximized at the lower end of this range. If the fraction of bad types is too low, there is no credible punishment for defection, and cooperation unravels. If too high, the probability of forming a cooperative relationship is too low, and defection dominates. The model also characterizes mixed strategy equilibria and shows that welfare can drop discontinuously as parameters cross critical thresholds. The main outcome is social welfare (efficiency), which is explicitly calculated as a function of the type distribution and model parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that endogenous social structure (relationship formation and dissolution) can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency, but only under certain conditions on the population's type distribution. The efficiency of the 'treatment' (i.e., the equilibrium with endogenous punishment via exclusion) is maximized when the fraction of bad types is just high enough to make the threat of exclusion credible, but not so high as to make cooperation infeasible. The model does not map directly onto standard public goods games with explicit punishment options, but it is highly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated games with endogenous partner choice or ostracism mechanisms. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency is non-monotonic and highly sensitive to the underlying population structure. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the presence and credibility of punishment (even if not costly or explicit) can have strong, but non-linear, effects on efficiency.

- source: Fujiwara-Greve, T., Okuno-Fujiwara, M., & Suzuki, N. (2015). Efficiency may improve when defectors exist. *Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with endogenous partner choice and no information flow, the most efficient Nash equilibrium is not the symmetric trust-building equilibrium (where all players initially defect to build trust), but rather a bimorphic equilibrium where some players always cooperate and others always defect and leave. This equilibrium is locally stable and, under a 'small stake' condition (when the temptation to defect is not too large), is more efficient than any equilibrium with only trust-building strategies. The efficiency gain comes from allowing a higher share of cooperators, even though some are exploited by defectors. The paper provides explicit conditions for when this efficiency dominance holds and characterizes the parameter regions where the presence of defectors improves efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environments with endogenous partner choice and no information flow, the introduction of unconditional defectors can increase equilibrium efficiency compared to trust-building (punishment-based) equilibria, especially when the temptation to defect is not too large (small stake condition) and the effective discount factor (survival rate) is high. The efficiency of the equilibrium is explicitly characterized as a function of the payoff parameters and the share of cooperators/defectors. While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not feature explicit costly punishment, it is highly relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated games with endogenous partner selection and limited punishment options. The results suggest that, under certain conditions, the presence of defectors can be efficiency-improving, and that efficiency does not always monotonically increase with the strength or prevalence of punishment strategies.

- source: Ochea, M. I. (2013). Evolution of repeated prisoner's dilemma play under logit dynamics. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with a finite set of memory-one strategies, the long-run average payoff (efficiency) and the prevalence of cooperation depend critically on the mix of strategies present and the game parameters. In some ecologies, strategies like Pavlov (WSLS), which can be interpreted as implementing endogenous punishment (by defecting after being defected upon), can dominate and sustain high payoffs, especially when unconditional cooperators are absent. However, the introduction of unconditional cooperators (AllC) can destabilize cooperation and lead to the dominance of defectors (AllD), reducing efficiency. The dynamics can be complex, with cycles and chaos emerging for intermediate values of the intensity of choice parameter. The paper provides explicit formulas for average payoffs and analyzes bifurcations in the system as parameters change. Overall, the effect of 'punishment-like' strategies on efficiency is mixed and highly sensitive to the strategic environment and parameter values.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like strategies (e.g., Pavlov/WSLS) on efficiency in repeated social dilemma games is highly context-dependent. In environments without unconditional cooperators, such strategies can sustain cooperation and high payoffs, but their effectiveness is undermined when unconditional cooperators are present. The results suggest that the presence or absence of certain strategy types (especially unconditional cooperators) and the values of key parameters (benefit of cooperation, error rates, intensity of choice) are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of enabling punishment (or punishment-like strategies) on efficiency cannot be determined solely by the presence of punishment, but must account for the broader strategic ecology and parameter settings. The findings are most directly relevant to repeated PD-like environments, but may inform predictions in PGG-like settings by analogy.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2024). The universal probabilistic reward based on the difference of payoff realizes the evolution of cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a universal probabilistic reward mechanism, where players with higher payoffs reward their neighbors (cooperators or defectors) with a probability proportional to the payoff difference, leads to a significant increase in both cooperation and average group payoff (efficiency) in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. The effect is robust across different network topologies (regular, random, scale-free) and a range of reward coefficients (t), except when t=0 or t=1, where the mechanism is inactive. The study provides detailed analysis of how the speed and stability of cooperation and efficiency depend on the reward parameter and network structure. No punishment mechanism is implemented in the model, but the paper situates its contribution in the context of prior work on both reward and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that a universal, payoff-difference-based reward mechanism can substantially increase group efficiency in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, even when rewards are given to both cooperators and defectors. While the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, it is highly informative for predicting the effects of reward-based interventions on efficiency, especially in networked or spatially structured games. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, well-designed reward mechanisms can achieve high efficiency. However, the results may not directly transfer to public goods games with explicit punishment options, as the model does not include a punishment treatment or control. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that reward mechanisms (especially those not limited to cooperators) can be highly effective at increasing efficiency, but does not provide direct evidence about the marginal effect of adding punishment to a baseline.

- source: Gao, L., Li, Y. T., & Wang, R. W. (2015). The shift between the Red Queen and the Red King effects in mutualisms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in multiplayer snowdrift games modeling mutualisms, the introduction and increase of a collective reward mechanism can shift the allocation of mutualistic benefits from favoring the faster-evolving species (Red Queen effect) to favoring the slower-evolving species (Red King effect). Asymmetries in the number of interacting partners or in payoffs can also shift the balance of surplus allocation. The model predicts that, depending on the reward intensity and asymmetries, the efficiency (total surplus generated and its division) can be increased or made more unequal. The model is theoretical and does not provide empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the presence and intensity of a reward mechanism (analogous to a positive incentive or bonus for cooperation) can substantially alter the efficiency and allocation of surplus in multiplayer social dilemmas. While it does not directly model punishment or public goods games, its results suggest that reward mechanisms can increase group efficiency and shift the distribution of payoffs, especially in the presence of asymmetries. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that enabling a reward mechanism (with sufficient intensity) can increase efficiency and alter the distribution of payoffs, but the effect depends on the details of the reward and the structure of the game (e.g., group size, asymmetries). The findings are most relevant for settings with reward rather than punishment, and for snowdrift-like rather than public-goods-like games.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2023). Grouping promotes both partnership and rivalry with long memory in direct reciprocity. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The main finding is that group structure, when combined with the availability of longer-memory strategies (which allow for friendly rival strategies), leads to the evolution of nearly full cooperation and maximal efficiency in the repeated donation game. In well-mixed populations, longer memory does not have this effect. The results are robust to the benefit-to-cost ratio and to details of the evolutionary process. The paper does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms, but the repeated donation game is adjacent to PGGs, and friendly rival strategies function as a form of endogenous sanctioning (never allowing the co-player to earn more). Efficiency is measured as the fraction of time mutual cooperation is achieved, which is equivalent to group payoff relative to the maximum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player repeated donation games (adjacent to PGGs), the combination of group structure and the availability of longer-memory strategies (enabling friendly rival strategies) can dramatically increase efficiency, even when cooperation would otherwise be difficult. While there is no explicit punishment mechanism, friendly rival strategies act as endogenous enforcers of cooperation by never allowing the co-player to earn more. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in environments with group structure and sufficient strategic complexity, efficiency can be much higher than in well-mixed or memory-limited settings, even without explicit punishment. However, the results may not transfer directly to multi-player PGGs or to settings with explicit costly punishment.

- source: Trejo, K. K., Juarez, R., Clempner, J. B., & Poznyak, A. S. (2023). Non-Cooperative Bargaining with Unsophisticated Agents. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing penalty mechanisms for deviation and time in bargaining games with myopic agents ensures convergence to efficient (Pareto optimal or Nash) outcomes, but at a cost: the process is slower and less efficient than with fully rational, forward-looking agents. The efficiency loss is quantifiable as the 'price of unsophistication,' and depends on the penalty parameters and the agents' behavior. The results are robust across a wide class of utility functions and game structures, including Markov processes. The paper does not study public goods games or peer punishment, but the penalty mechanism is analogous to centralized sanctions rather than decentralized peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of peer punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies bargaining games with centralized penalty mechanisms and myopic agents. However, it provides indirect evidence that introducing penalty costs can improve convergence to efficient outcomes even with unsophisticated agents, though always with some efficiency loss relative to the ideal. The results suggest that the design and magnitude of penalties (punishment costs) are critical for achieving near-efficient outcomes, but the context is bargaining, not public goods provision. The findings may be useful for understanding the general role of sanctions in promoting efficiency, but should be transferred to PGG-like settings with caution.

- source: Iwamura, Y., Nagashima, K., & Tanimoto, J. (2020). Evolutionary dynamics of a 3-strategy game: Cooperator, defector and costly cooperative loner strategic types. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The introduction of a costly cooperative loner (CL) as a third strategy in a prisoner's dilemma game does not prevent the collapse to all-defection, nor does it improve social average payoff (efficiency) compared to the standard two-strategy game. The CL strategy is always outcompeted and goes extinct, after which defectors dominate. This is in contrast to models with costly punishment, where punishment can sometimes sustain cooperation and higher efficiency. The paper provides a detailed analysis of the payoff matrix and evolutionary trajectories, showing that the addition of CL does not alter the efficiency outcome: the system always ends in the least efficient (all-defector) state.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a negative case: simply adding a third strategy that is not an active punisher (but pays a cost to avoid defectors) does not improve efficiency in a prisoner's dilemma environment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a 'costly loner' or similar non-punishing third strategy is not sufficient to increase efficiency. The results reinforce that only certain types of third strategies (e.g., active costly punishment or rewarding) can shift efficiency outcomes. The findings are most relevant for infinite, well-mixed populations and may not generalize to finite or structured populations. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment per se, but its comparison to punishment models supports the view that punishment (with sufficient impact and cost structure) is necessary for efficiency gains in these settings.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2007). Indirect reciprocity in asymmetric interactions: when apparent altruism facilitates profitable exploitation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric games where only one party (the actor) can help or exploit, and the other (the recipient) can only choose whether to interact, a form of indirect reciprocity can evolve. Actors help when the gains from exploitation are low to maintain a positive image, which encourages recipients to approach them in the future, allowing profitable exploitation when the gains are high. This strategy is stable and increases the long-term average payoff for both actors and recipients, provided certain conditions on observability, state-switching, and payoff parameters are met. The model shows that efficiency (average group payoff) is higher under these conditions than if actors always exploit or if recipients always avoid interaction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in asymmetric games with image scoring and conditional approach, mechanisms analogous to punishment (here, avoidance of exploiters) can increase efficiency by supporting conditional cooperation. The findings suggest that when harmful behavior is observable and actors' states vary, efficiency is maximized for intermediate values of key parameters (cost of harm, switching probability, observability). While not a PGG or direct punishment setting, the model is adjacent and supports the idea that social mechanisms that deter exploitation (even without direct punishment) can improve group efficiency. For prediction, the paper suggests that enabling mechanisms for reputation-based avoidance or indirect sanctions can increase efficiency, especially when exploitation is not always profitable and can be detected.

- source: Auerswald, H., Schmidt, C., Thum, M., & Torsvik, G. (2018). Teams in a public goods experiment with punishment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The evolutionary IPD with choice and refusal of partners (IPD/CR) produces higher average fitness (group efficiency) than random partner matching, with most runs evolving to near full cooperation. The average fitness clusters around a few regions, with the highest corresponding to mutual cooperation. However, if the refusal payoff is high or players are highly intolerant of defection, populations can become trapped in low-efficiency wallflower states. Sensitivity analyses show that the refusal payoff, minimum tolerance level, and memory weight are key moderators: high refusal payoffs or high intolerance increase the risk of social isolation and lower efficiency. Increasing population size reduces the risk of wallflower ecologies. The findings are robust across different strategy representations and parameter sweeps.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling partner selection (choice and refusal) in repeated social dilemmas can increase group efficiency compared to random matching, especially when refusal payoffs and intolerance are not too high. For prediction tasks, the presence of a partner selection mechanism (even in the absence of explicit costly punishment) can be expected to increase efficiency, but only if the cost/benefit structure of refusal does not incentivize social isolation. The results are most directly relevant to settings where agents can avoid defectors, but less so to standard public goods games with centralized punishment. The findings suggest that game design dimensions such as the cost of refusal, tolerance thresholds, and population size are critical moderators of the efficiency effect.

- source: Lee, S., Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2021). Local stability of cooperation in a continuous model of indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of cooperation in a continuous donation game with indirect reciprocity depends on the sensitivity of the assessment and behavioral rules (social norms). It derives explicit conditions (in terms of derivatives of the assessment and behavioral functions) under which a cooperative norm is stable against local mutants, and shows that the benefit-to-cost ratio required for stability increases if reputation is insensitive to observed behavior or if cooperation is insensitive to recipient reputation. The analysis is supported by numerical simulations, which confirm the theoretical predictions about payoff differences between resident and mutant norms. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms as in standard PGGs, but reputation loss functions as an indirect sanction. The main outcome is the expected payoff (efficiency) of the group under different norms and parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence about how the structure of social norms (in terms of sensitivity to behavior and reputation) affects the efficiency (payoff) of cooperation in a continuous donation game with indirect reciprocity. While not a direct PGG or explicit punishment game, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where cooperation is enforced by reputation-based indirect sanctions rather than direct punishment. The findings suggest that the efficiency of cooperation depends on the responsiveness of the reputation system and the behavioral rule, and that certain parameter regimes (high benefit-to-cost ratio, high sensitivity) are required for stable, efficient cooperation. The results are most applicable to settings where indirect reciprocity is the main enforcement mechanism, and less so to direct punishment or reward treatments in PGGs.

- source: Forges, F., Horst, U., & Salomon, A. (2016). Feasibility and individual rationality in two-person Bayesian games. *International Journal of Game Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in two-person Bayesian games with a single informed player, the existence of a uniform punishment strategy (UPS) for the uninformed player guarantees the existence of feasible, individually rational (i.e., efficient/welfare-maximizing) solutions. These solutions can be implemented as Nash equilibria of appropriately designed noncooperative games. The set of such payoffs coincides with the set of joint plan equilibrium payoffs in the infinitely repeated game. When UPS does not hold, feasible, individually rational solutions may not exist. The results generalize to cases where both players have private information, under certain conditions. The paper provides theoretical foundations for how punishment strategies can support efficient outcomes in games with incomplete information, but does not provide empirical estimates or experimental results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the ability to punish (specifically, the existence of a uniform punishment strategy) is crucial for achieving efficient, individually rational outcomes in two-person Bayesian games with incomplete information. For the downstream prediction task, the main implication is that the presence and effectiveness of punishment mechanisms (punishment cost, punishment tech, and the ability to credibly threaten punishment) are key moderators of whether efficiency gains from punishment are possible. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical estimates, and its results are not specific to public goods games or experimental designs. It is most useful for understanding the theoretical boundary conditions under which punishment can support efficiency, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency changes in experimental PGG-like environments.

- source: Ille, S. (2021). The evolution of sectarianism. *Communications in Nonlinear Science and Numerical Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of coercive (punishment-like) strategies in a repeated bargaining game can lead to either efficient egalitarian outcomes or inefficient sectarian contracts, depending on the relative costs and benefits of coercion and the structure of social learning. When the cost of coercion is low and potential gains from inegalitarian contracts are high, societies are more likely to settle into inefficient, conflictual, or sectarian equilibria. When both groups have similar coercive capacities and the cost of coercion is high, egalitarian and efficient outcomes are more likely. The model also shows that collective memory, identity politics, and initial asymmetries can tip the balance toward persistent conflict or sectarianism, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (coercion) on efficiency is highly context-dependent. If punishment is symmetric and costly, it can support efficient cooperation (egalitarian contracts). If punishment is asymmetric or the gains from inegalitarian outcomes are high, punishment can entrench inefficient, conflictual, or exploitative equilibria. The model suggests that game design dimensions such as the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the symmetry of player power, and the structure of social learning critically moderate the efficiency impact of punishment. However, the model is not a direct PGG or public goods game, but rather a repeated bargaining/coordination game with endogenous punishment-like coercion. Thus, its findings are most relevant for adjacent settings where punishment can be used to enforce either efficient or inefficient group outcomes.

- source: Reiersen, J. (2017). TRUST AS A BOOSTER. *Journal of Business Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that trust is essential for enabling players to leave secure networks and engage in market transactions that offer higher potential payoffs but also greater risk. The model shows that the willingness to transact in markets depends on the expected probability of encountering trustworthy partners, the relative payoffs from network versus market transactions, and the cost of being cheated. When trust is high and/or the gains from market transactions are large relative to networks, players are more likely to leave networks and realize higher efficiency (total payoff). Conversely, when trust is low or the cost of being cheated is high, players remain in networks, foregoing potential efficiency gains. The model highlights the distinction between trust (needed for market transactions) and assurance (provided by networks through repeated interaction and informal sanctions).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how efficiency (expected group payoff) in environments with and without trust/assurance mechanisms depends on the structure of payoffs, risk, and expectations about others' behavior. While it does not directly model public goods games or experimental punishment, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where players can choose between secure, repeated interactions (with informal sanctions) and riskier, anonymous interactions (requiring trust). The model suggests that efficiency gains from moving to market-like environments depend on the level of trust and the relative payoffs, and that mechanisms providing assurance (such as punishment or repeated interaction) can substitute for trust in supporting cooperation. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment in public goods games, so its relevance for predicting the effect of enabling punishment is indirect and primarily theoretical.

- source: Phillips, T., Li, J. W., & Kendall, G. (2014). The Effects of Extra-Somatic Weapons on the Evolution of Human Cooperation towards Non-Kin. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Simulating a population of 40 players using 25 well-known IPD strategies, the authors compare two conditions: a standard evolutionary IPD and a 'weapons use' IPD where repeated defections trigger a risk of elimination (interpreted as a proxy for lethal punishment or high-cost disputes). Cooperative strategies, on average, survive significantly longer under weapons use, while non-cooperative strategies survive for fewer generations. The 'Always Cooperate' strategy, typically the least successful, becomes the most successful cooperative strategy as the risk of elimination increases. The model suggests that high costs for repeated defection (analogous to punishment) can shift evolutionary success toward cooperation, even in the absence of explicit monitoring or repeated interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that increasing the cost of within-group conflict (modeled as a risk of elimination after repeated defections) can increase the evolutionary success of cooperative strategies and decrease that of non-cooperative strategies. While the model is not a public goods game and does not report group efficiency per se, the findings are relevant for predicting that introducing or increasing the severity of punishment in repeated social dilemmas is likely to increase the relative payoff or evolutionary success of cooperation. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment is severe and unavoidable, and less so to standard PGGs with explicit, voluntary punishment stages. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of specific game design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, chat), but supports the general principle that costly sanctions can promote cooperation and group-level success.

- source: Huang, S. X., Liu, X. S., Hu, Y. H., & Fu, X. (2023). The influence of aggressive behavior on cooperation evolution in social dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing aggressive behavior (a form of costly punishment) into a prisoner's dilemma game can promote the persistence of cooperation by deterring defectors. Aggressive cooperators are able to suppress defectors, especially when the benefit of aggression (compensation) is high and the risk (mutual loss) is not too great. The system can reach various equilibria, including coexistence of multiple strategies, depending on parameter values. When the temptation to defect is low and aggressive compensation is high, cooperation dominates. As the temptation to defect increases, defectors re-emerge, but aggressive defectors can also help limit the dominance of non-aggressive defectors, allowing some cooperation to survive. The findings suggest that punishment-like mechanisms can improve group outcomes compared to a baseline with only cooperation and defection, though the paper does not quantify efficiency in the standard sense.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that introducing a punishment-like mechanism (aggressive behavior) in a prisoner's dilemma environment can increase group payoff and support cooperation, especially when the cost-benefit tradeoff of punishment is favorable and the temptation to defect is not too high. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (with moderate cost and sufficient effectiveness) is likely to improve efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, particularly in environments where the risk of mutual punishment is not prohibitive. However, the lack of explicit efficiency ratios and the focus on evolutionary stability rather than direct payoff comparisons limit the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Li, S. J., Bao, W., Dai, Y. Y., Ye, Y., & Xie, N. G. (2022). Emotional game and the evolution of cooperation considering the effects of reputation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that adding a reputation mechanism based on emotional profiles to a voluntary prisoner's dilemma game (VPDG) substantially increases both the frequency of cooperation and the average payoff of the population. The reputation mechanism works by making individuals more likely to cooperate with those who have good reputations and less likely to cooperate with those who have bad reputations, effectively integrating both reward and punishment effects. Compared to previous models (VPDG alone and VPDG with emotion but no reputation), the model with reputation achieves higher average payoffs and more stable cooperation, especially in regions of the parameter space where cooperation would otherwise be low. The effect is strongest when the temptation to defect is not too high (T < 1.5), but even at higher temptations, the reputation mechanism maintains higher cooperation and payoff than the baselines.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that mechanisms integrating both reward and punishment via reputation can substantially increase group efficiency in voluntary public-goods-like games. While the model is not a standard PGG, the adjacent structure (VPDG with loner option) and the explicit reporting of average payoff as a welfare/efficiency measure make the findings relevant for predicting the effect of adding punishment/reward/reputation mechanisms in similar environments. The results suggest that, when the control (no reputation) game has moderate or low efficiency, enabling a reputation mechanism (which includes both positive and negative sanctions) can drive efficiency close to the cooperative maximum, especially when the temptation to defect is not extreme. However, the findings are based on simulation, not human experiments, and the specific implementation of reputation is more complex than simple punishment. The model does not directly manipulate all design dimensions (e.g., player count, chat, etc.), so predictions should be adjusted accordingly.

- source: Milinski, M. (2022). Extortion - A voracious prosocial strategy. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that extortionate strategies in iterated Prisoner's Dilemma games can enforce high cooperation from coplayers, but at the cost of an unequal payoff distribution favoring the extortioner. When coplayers attempt to punish extortioners by withholding cooperation, both parties' payoffs decrease, demonstrating a form of costly punishment. In symmetric settings, this can discipline extortioners, but in asymmetric settings (e.g., with replacement power or extra incentives), extortioners can persist and maintain higher payoffs. The review highlights that the efficiency of the group (total payoff) may not increase with extortion, as the gains are captured by the extortioner and punishment reduces total surplus. The findings are based on a synthesis of theory and several experimental studies, but the review itself does not report new experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, costly punishment via withholding cooperation) on efficiency depends on the symmetry of the game and the power structure. In symmetric games, punishment can reduce the extortioner's advantage but at a cost to both players, lowering efficiency. In asymmetric games, extortioners can avoid being disciplined and extract higher payoffs, further reducing the coplayer's share. The review does not directly address public goods games or classic punishment institutions, but the mechanisms discussed (costly punishment, power asymmetry, incentive structures) are relevant for predicting when punishment will improve or reduce efficiency in related environments. The evidence is more about distributional outcomes and the limits of punishment than about average efficiency increases from punishment.

- source: Rankin, D. J., & Eggimann, F. (2009). The evolution of judgement bias in indirect reciprocity. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that judgement bias—weighting bad acts more heavily than good acts in reputation updating—can evolve in indirect reciprocity models, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is low. Under 'standing' reputation rules, negative judgement bias is favored over a wider range of parameters than under 'scoring.' Allowing judgement bias to evolve increases the level of cooperation in the population. The model does not include explicit costly punishment, but negative judgement bias acts as a form of social sanctioning by reducing the likelihood of future help to defectors. The model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it suggests that mechanisms that increase the reputational cost of defection (such as negative judgement bias) can promote cooperation, especially when the cost of cooperation is high. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation-based indirect reciprocity is the main mechanism, rather than explicit costly punishment. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Bruner, J. P., & Smead, R. (2022). Tag-based spite with correlated interactions. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of observable tags and the possibility of conditioning behavior on tags (e.g., only harming out-group members) fundamentally changes the evolutionary dynamics of costly harming (spite) in the Prisoner's Delight game. Tags make pure (unconditional) spite less likely to evolve even when anti-correlation would otherwise favor it, but make conditional (tag-based) spite more likely in some regimes. Several stable equilibria are identified, including cooperative polymorphisms (mostly cooperation, little spite), pure spite, and a novel 'treacherous polymorphism' where spite is directed within groups. The presence of tags generally reduces the prevalence of widespread spiteful behavior, even under conditions that would otherwise favor it. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and behavioral frequencies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The model is a two-player game (Prisoner's Delight) with costly harming, not a multi-player public goods game. The main outcomes are the evolutionary frequencies of behavioral types, not group efficiency or total payoff. However, the analysis is relevant for understanding how the structure of punishment-like mechanisms (costly harming, conditional on observable tags) and population structure (correlated/anti-correlated interactions) can affect the prevalence of cooperation and spite. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing for conditional punishment (e.g., based on group identity) can both promote and suppress harmful behavior, depending on the context. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect theoretical context about how punishment-like behaviors may interact with population structure and signaling, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Hu, Q., Jin, T., Jiang, Y. L., & Liu, X. W. (2024). Reputation incentives with public supervision promote cooperation in evolutionary games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that a reputation incentive mechanism with public supervision can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG. The effect is stronger when the evaluation intensity (the range of possible evaluation values) is lower, allowing players to more quickly update their evaluations and reputations. The model demonstrates that reputation-based rewards can compensate cooperators for losses due to defectors, promoting the formation and persistence of cooperative clusters.
