- source: Zhang, C. Y., Li, Q. Y., Zhu, Y. Y., Han, J. D., & Zhang, J. L. (2020). Evolutionary dynamics in division of labor games on cycle networks. *European Journal of Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that in division of labor games on cycle networks, the update rule (BD, DB, or mixed) has a major impact on the speed at which efficient strategies (interpreted as high-quality task allocation) spread through the population. Under strong selection, BD updating leads to fixation times that scale linearly with population size, while DB updating leads to quadratic or cubic scaling, meaning much slower convergence. The mixed rule interpolates between these extremes. The fixation probability and time depend on the payoff matrix parameters, but the presence or absence of punishment is not considered. The results are relevant for understanding how structural and dynamic features of the game affect the emergence of efficient collective behavior, but do not directly address efficiency as a payoff ratio or the effect of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. However, it does offer indirect insight into how update rules and network structure can affect the speed and likelihood of efficient collective behavior emerging in games with a cooperative dilemma structure. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding baseline dynamics in the absence of punishment and for considering the role of update mechanisms and network topology.

- source: Chen, W. M., Quan, J., Wang, X. J., & Liu, Y. (2023). Evolutionary dynamics from fluctuating environments with deterministic and stochastic noises. *Nonlinear Dynamics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper develops a theoretical model of evolutionary dynamics in the hawk-dove game under fluctuating environments with both payoff and demographic noise. Through simulation, it finds that both deterministic and stochastic fluctuations can promote cooperation, sometimes even reversing selection so that cooperation dominates. Deterministic fluctuations are more effective than stochastic ones at promoting cooperation. The mechanism is that noise can create unfair time-averaged payoffs that favor cooperation. High variance in noise promotes cooperation but makes its maintenance less stable. The results are robust to the type of noise distribution. The paper briefly extends the analysis to the prisoner's dilemma, finding similar qualitative results. No public goods game, punishment, or explicit group efficiency analysis is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not study public goods games, punishment, or group efficiency. Its findings about noise and cooperation in the hawk-dove and prisoner's dilemma games may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how environmental uncertainty affects cooperation, but not for the specific prediction task involving punishment and efficiency.

- source: Podobnik, B., Jusup, M., Wang, Z., & Stanley, H. E. (2017). How Fear of Future Outcomes Affects Social Dynamics. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of benevolence (and thus group-level efficiency or welfare) in an open society depends on the interplay between insiders' tolerance, the assimilation rate of outsiders, and the network structure of interactions. As the fraction of outsiders increases, benevolence (measured by the fitness ratio R) generally decreases. If insiders' tolerance is too low or assimilation is too slow, the system can experience a sudden breakdown of cooperation (herd behavior), leading to antagonism and loss of mutual benefit. If tolerance and assimilation are balanced, a stable mutualistic equilibrium can be maintained. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it does provide indirect insight into how the withdrawal of benevolence (analogous to defection or exclusion) can destabilize cooperation and reduce group welfare. The model's outcomes are determined by tolerance and assimilation parameters, not by punishment or reward. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Geng, Y. N., Lu, Y. K., Hong, L. J., & Shi, L. (2025). Evolution of cooperation with asymmetric rewards. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that asymmetric rewards in a two-population prisoner's dilemma model can sustain cooperation and prevent total defection, especially in structured populations (lattices). The effect of asymmetry on cooperation depends on the dilemma strength: under strong dilemma (high temptation to defect), asymmetry promotes cooperation; under weak dilemma, strong asymmetry can actually inhibit cooperation. The model includes both prosocial and antisocial rewards but does not include punishment or sanctions. The outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates and the coexistence of different strategies, not in terms of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not study punishment, public goods games, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings on asymmetric rewards in the prisoner's dilemma may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how positive incentives (rewards) can sustain cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment or efficiency. The game structure, outcome measures, and intervention type (reward, not punishment) are all different from the target prediction context.

The model does not include punishment or negative sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The game structure is adjacent to, but not the same as, a public goods game, and the outcome measures are not payoff-based.

  findings: The paper finds that the equilibrium level of cooperation in a population depends on the distribution of agents' emotional parameters (guilt and especially betrayal). Increasing the number of agents with a lower betrayal factor does not always increase cooperation; there are critical points where the population becomes robust to further increases. The models show that the equilibrium cooperation rate can be predicted from the distribution of emotional types, but the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment. The findings are relevant for understanding how emotional payoffs shape cooperation in social dilemmas, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The study finds that in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game where players can adopt different strategies toward each neighbor (interactive diversity) and update their strategies based on an aspiration threshold, the fraction of cooperation (F_c) can be significantly increased by tuning the aspiration parameter (alpha). There is an optimal range of aspiration for each level of temptation to defect (b), and too high or too low aspiration can suppress cooperation. The results are presented as phase diagrams and time series of F_c, but no group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes are reported. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are included in the model.

  findings: The paper finds that preferential selection (aspiring to the most successful neighbor, i.e., positive w) promotes cooperation in the voluntary PDG for low temptation to defect (small b), but this effect is suppressed as b increases. - source: Li, S. L., Hong, L. J., Geng, Y. N., & Shen, C. (2020). Popularity-driven fitness calculation promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that integrating popularity into the fitness function (via a tunable parameter alpha) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can significantly promote cooperation. When alpha is positive, popular individuals (those whose neighbors share their strategy) gain higher fitness, leading to the formation and expansion of cooperative clusters and, in some cases, full cooperation. When alpha is negative, isolated cooperators can persist but do not dominate. The results are supported by both numerical simulations and mean-field theoretical analysis. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, nor does it include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that mechanisms which enhance the fitness of popular strategies can promote cooperation, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment. The game studied is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the outcome of interest is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency.

- source: He, J. Z., Zhao, Y., Cai, H. J., & Wang, R. W. (2013). Spatial games and the maintenance of cooperation in an asymmetric Hawk-Dove game. *Chinese Science Bulletin*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial asymmetric Hawk-Dove game, the probability of cooperation by strong players increases as the benefit-to-cost ratio decreases, while weak players tend to defect. When the benefit-to-cost ratio is sufficiently small, cooperation by strong players dominates. The degree of asymmetry also affects the distribution of strategies: higher asymmetry reduces defection by strong players but increases defection by weak players. The system can exhibit chaotic or oscillating patterns in strategy frequencies, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is near a threshold. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not include punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings on how benefit-to-cost ratio and asymmetry affect cooperation and defection frequencies in a spatial Hawk-Dove game may be of indirect, adjacent interest for understanding cooperation dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Zhang, S. H., Zhang, Z. P., Wu, Y. E., Li, Y., & Xie, Y. Y. (2018). Coevolution of teaching ability and cooperation in spatial evolutionary games. *Scientific Reports*.

  findings: The study finds that introducing a dynamic, reputation-based teaching ability mechanism into spatial evolutionary games (PDG and SDG) significantly increases the fraction of cooperators across a wide range of parameters and network structures. The mechanism works by making players more likely to adopt strategies from neighbors with higher reputation, which itself is updated based on cooperative or defective actions. The effect is robust to changes in network topology and game type. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.

The game structure is also not a public goods game but rather spatial PDG and SDG. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs.

- source: Lu, P. (2015). Learn good from bad: Effects of good and bad neighbors in spatial prisoners' dilemma games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with agent heterogeneity, commons are more likely to cooperate when surrounded by more bad neighbors (scoundrels) and less likely to cooperate when surrounded by more good neighbors (elites). The effect of neighbor type is modulated by the temptation to defect (parameter b). The model does not include punishment, reward, or explicit sanctioning mechanisms. The main outcome is the cooperation rate of commons, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings about the effect of heterogeneous neighbors on cooperation rates in a spatial prisoner's dilemma may be of limited, indirect relevance for understanding how environmental structure or agent heterogeneity affects cooperation, but not for the specific prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Lu, K., Wu, B., Li, M. C., & Wang, Z. (2014). Other-regarding preference causing ping-pong effect in self-questioning game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the weight of other-regarding preferences (alpha) promotes cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with synchronous updating. For low alpha, cooperation collapses; for high alpha, cooperation dominates. At intermediate alpha, a 'ping-pong effect' emerges, with large fluctuations in the fraction of cooperators. The study analyzes the micro-dynamics of strategy switching but does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes, and the game structure is a spatial prisoner's dilemma with a self-questioning update rule, not a public goods game. The findings about other-regarding preferences and cooperation rates may be contextually informative for understanding how social preferences affect cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Du, F. Q., & Fu, F. (2013). Quantifying the impact of noise on macroscopic organization of cooperation in spatial games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that the effect of noise (randomness in strategy updating) on cooperation and spatial assortment differs between the spatial prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games. In the prisoner's dilemma, intermediate noise maximizes local assortment and cooperator cluster size, while in the snowdrift game, noise generally weakens assortment and leads to smaller clusters at intermediate noise. The paper does not address public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is relevant only in that it discusses spatial structure and noise in related two-player games (prisoner's dilemma, snowdrift), but does not include group interactions, punishment, or payoff-based efficiency outcomes. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Liao, H. M., Hao, Q. Y., Qian, J. L., Wu, C. Y., Guo, N., & Ling, X. (2023). Cooperative evolution under the joint influence of local popularity and global popularity. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that the weight of local popularity and the scaling index of popularity in the fitness function can significantly affect the steady-state fraction of cooperators in a spatial WPDG. When the scaling index is positive, high weight on local popularity promotes cooperation, while global popularity is detrimental. When the scaling index is negative, global popularity can help small clusters of cooperators survive even in harsh environments. There is no analysis of punishment, reward, or group efficiency/payoff. The results are entirely about the prevalence of cooperation, not about group welfare or surplus.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. It does not include punishment, reward, or any efficiency/welfare outcome. Its findings about local/global popularity and cooperation rates in spatial WPDG are not transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Lu, P. (2015). Individual choice and reputation distribution of cooperative behaviors among heterogeneous groups. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that the mean of reputation (interpreted as a proxy for cooperation) declines with increasing temptation (b), and is negatively affected by the proportion of elites and positively by the proportion of scoundrels. The diversity (standard deviation) of reputation increases with temptation and is affected in a quadratic manner by the proportion of elites and the local cooperation ratio. However, the paper does not study public goods games, punishment, or efficiency/payoff outcomes. All results are based on simulation of a spatial prisoner's dilemma with three agent types and reputation updating.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its findings on reputation and cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with heterogeneous agents may be of limited adjacent interest for understanding micro-level cooperation dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task.

- source: Sekiguchi, T. (2013). General conditions for strategy abundance through a self-referential mechanism under weak selection. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that, under weak selection, the self-referential updating rule (where players are more likely to abandon their strategy if dissatisfied with their payoff) leads to a greater difference in the stationary frequencies of favored versus unfavored strategies than the standard Moran process. This holds for both symmetric and asymmetric games. When mutation rates are small, the two updating rules yield nearly identical results. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not address efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or sanctions. The results are relevant for understanding how different evolutionary updating rules affect the abundance of strategies, but not for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or punishment games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence or guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, nor does it model punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its results are limited to the distribution of strategies under different evolutionary updating rules. Therefore, it should not be used as a primary source for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions or control efficiency in the context of punishment interventions.

- source: Clempner, J. B. (2018). A continuous-time Markov Stackelberg security game approach for reasoning about real patrol strategies. *International Journal of Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper presents a novel continuous-time Markov Stackelberg game model for security resource allocation, providing a method to compute equilibrium strategies that minimize average cost for defenders (leaders) and attackers (followers). The approach is shown to be computationally tractable and more realistic for real-world patrolling scenarios than previous discrete-time models. A numerical example demonstrates the convergence of strategies and the ability of the defender to eventually capture the attacker, but all results are theoretical or simulation-based and not tied to public goods or punishment in the sense of PGGs.

Its relevance is limited to the general modeling of adversarial resource allocation and efficiency/cost outcomes in security games. The game structure, outcomes, and mechanisms differ substantially from PGGs with or without punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, except possibly as very weak, adjacent evidence about the optimization of efficiency in adversarial settings.
